=49=2*5² -1. So according to this, p=5 would be a solution. But wait, when we computed (7^4 -1)/5=2400/5=480, which is not a square. There is a contradiction here.

Wait, this suggests a mistake in the logic. Let me retrace. 

From the original equation: (7^{p-1} -1)/p =k². For p=5, this is (2401 -1)/5=2400/5=480. 480 is not a perfect square. However, according to the Case2 analysis, we get that k² -5 m²=1 with x=7^{(5-1)/2}=49=2k² -1, which gives k=5. So 2k² -1=49, correct. But then according to the Case2, ab= n² p. Let's check.

When p=5, x=49.

x-1=48=2p m² ⇒48=2*5*m² ⇒24=5 m² ⇒m²=24/5, which is not an integer. This contradicts, so where is the mistake?

Wait, in the Case2: a=p m² and b=k². Then x-1=2a=2p m², x+1=2b=2k². So for p=5, x=49:

x-1=48=2*5*m² ⇒24=5*m² ⇒m²=24/5, which is not an integer. Therefore, the mistake was assuming that p=5 would be a solution. So even though the Pell equation is satisfied, the other equation (x-1=2p m²) gives a non-integer m. Therefore, the mistake was in the previous step when we thought that solving k² -p m²=1 would automatically give x=7^{...}, but actually, we have to check both equations. Therefore, even though in the case of p=5, there exists a solution to k² -5m²=1 (the Pell equation), but when we set x=7^{(p-1)/2}=49, the corresponding m would have to satisfy 48=2*5*m² ⇒m²=24/5, which is not integer. So p=5 is not a solution.

Therefore, the key is that even if the Pell equation has solutions, it's necessary that the specific value of x=7^{(p-1)/2} also satisfies the equation x=2k² -1, and simultaneously x-1=2p m². Therefore, both equations need to hold with integer m and k. So in the case of p=3, these equations hold with m=1 and k=2. Let's verify:

For p=3:

x=7^{(3-1)/2}=7^1=7.

From x=2k² -1: 7=2k² -1 ⇒2k²=8 ⇒k²=4 ⇒k=2.

From x-1=2p m²:7-1=6=2*3*m² ⇒6=6m² ⇒m²=1 ⇒m=1.

So both equations hold with integer m and k.

For p=5, even though solving the Pell equation k² -5m²=1 gives a solution (k=9, m=4), substituting into x=2k² -1=161, which is not 7^2=49. Therefore, this solution does not correspond to the x=7^{(p-1)/2}. Hence, even though Pell equations may have solutions, only those primes p where x=7^{(p-1)/2} satisfies x=2k² -1 and x-1=2p m² will work.

Therefore, we need to find primes p where 7^{(p-1)/2} +1 is twice a square, and 7^{(p-1)/2} -1 is twice p times a square.

Let me write it as:

7^{(p-1)/2} +1 = 2k²,

7^{(p-1)/2} -1 = 2p m².

Then, subtracting the two equations:

(7^{(p-1)/2} +1) - (7^{(p-1)/2} -1)=2k² -2p m² ⇒2=2(k² - p m²) ⇒1=k² - p m².

So we have the same Pell equation again. Thus, solutions must satisfy both equations. Therefore, the values of k and m are related via the Pell equation, but also must satisfy the original equations involving 7^{(p-1)/2}.

So to find such primes p, we can look for integers m, k, and primes p such that:

1. k² - p m² =1,

2. 7^{(p-1)/2} =2k² -1.

Alternatively, since 7^{(p-1)/2}=2k² -1, and k² =p m² +1 from the Pell equation, substitute k² into the first equation:

7^{(p-1)/2}=2(p m² +1) -1=2p m² +1.

Thus, 7^{(p-1)/2} =2p m² +1.

So the equation becomes:

7^{(p-1)/2} -2p m² =1.

We need to find primes p and integers m such that this holds.

Let me denote t=(p-1)/2. Then p=2t+1. The equation becomes:

7^{t} =2(2t+1)m² +1.

So, 7^{t} -1=2(2t+1)m².

We need to find integers t≥1 (since p is prime≥2, so t≥1), and m≥1, such that 7^t -1 is divisible by 2(2t+1), and the quotient is a perfect square.

So we can check for small t:

t=1:

7^1 -1=6=2*(2*1+1)*m² ⇒6=2*3*m² ⇒6=6m² ⇒m²=1 ⇒m=1. So m=1. So p=2*1+1=3. Which we know works.

t=2:

7^2 -1=49-1=48=2*(2*2+1)*m²=2*5*m²=10m² ⇒48=10m² ⇒m²=4.8, not integer. So no solution.

t=3:

7^3 -1=343 -1=342=2*(2*3+1)*m²=2*7*m²=14m² ⇒342=14m² ⇒m²=342/14=24.428... Not integer.

t=4:

7^4 -1=2401 -1=2400=2*(2*4+1)*m²=2*9*m²=18m² ⇒2400=18m² ⇒m²=2400/18≈133.333. Not integer.

t=5:

7^5=16807. 16807-1=16806=2*(2*5+1)*m²=2*11*m²=22m². 16806/22=763.909... Not integer.

t=6:

7^6=117649. 117649-1=117648=2*(2*6+1)*m²=2*13*m²=26m². 117648/26=4524.923... Not integer.

t=7:

7^7=823543. 823543-1=823542=2*(2*7+1)*m²=2*15*m²=30m². 823542/30=27451.4. Not integer.

t=8:

7^8=5764801. 5764801-1=5764800=2*(2*8+1)*m²=2*17*m²=34m². 5764800/34=169,552.941... Not integer.

t=9:

7^9=40353607. 40353607-1=40353606=2*(2*9+1)*m²=2*19*m²=38m². 40353606/38=1,062,042.26... Not integer.

t=10:

7^10=282475249. 28<#sep#>000 bytes (approx 320MB). That is acceptable for available memory (current coding contest memory limits are usually ~1-2 GB or higher). But whether the problem expects that.

Alternatively, maybe the constraints for n will be such that it's manageable, given n*q <=1e6.

But the code has to process.

Now, input handling:

v, x_0 y_0, x_1 y_1, ... x_{v-1} y_{v-1}

So, to read this, each property's data is read by first reading v as an integer.

Then, read 2*v numbers as the vertices, in order.

Thus, using scanf in C++ is possible but with more code, or using standard cin, but note that for large inputs it's better to use fast methods:

Probably best to read using scanf for doubles and integers.

Alternatively, in C++, read by sscanf-ing each line. But if there are many lines with a lot of numbers (e.g. a property with 40 vertices would have 81 elements in line), we need to read all elements.

Read the number v, then read 2*v numbers as a vector.

In C++ this can be done with istream.

  <#sep#>from torch.autograd import Function
import math
import torch
from torch import nn as nn
from torch.nn import functional as F
from torch.nn import init as init
from torchvision.models import vgg as vgg
import torch.utils.data
from torch.utils import data as data
from torch import autograd as autograd


def fused_leaky_relu(input, bias, negative_slope=0.2, scale=2 ** 0.5):
    return FusedLeakyReLUFunction.apply(input, bias, negative_slope, scale)


class FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, out, negative_slope, scale):
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        empty = grad_output.new_empty(0)
        grad_input = fused_act_ext.fused_bias_act(grad_output, empty, out, 
            3, 1, negative_slope, scale)
        dim = [0]
        if grad_input.ndim > 2:
            dim += list(range(2, grad_input.ndim))
        grad_bias = grad_input.sum(dim).detach()
        return grad_input, grad_bias

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input, gradgrad_bias):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_out = fused_act_ext.fused_bias_act(gradgrad_input,
            gradgrad_bias, out, 3, 1, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None


class FusedLeakyReLUFunction(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, bias, negative_slope, scale):
        empty = input.new_empty(0)
        out = fused_act_ext.fused_bias_act(input, bias, empty, 3, 0,
            negative_slope, scale)
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input, grad_bias = FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward.apply(
            grad_output, out, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return grad_input, grad_bias, None, None


class EqualLinear(nn.Module):
    """Equalized Linear as StyleGAN2.

    Args:
        in_channels (int): Size of each sample.
        out_channels (int): Size of each output sample.
        bias (bool): If set to ``False``, the layer will not learn an additive
            bias. Default: ``True``.
        bias_init_val (float): Bias initialized value. Default: 0.
        lr_mul (float): Learning rate multiplier. Default: 1.
        activation (None | str): The activation after ``linear`` operation.
            Supported: 'fused_lrelu', None. Default: None.
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, bias=True, bias_init_val=
        0, lr_mul=1, activation=None):
        super(EqualLinear, self).__init__()
        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.out_channels = out_channels
        self.lr_mul = lr_mul
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation not in ['fused_lrelu', None]:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Wrong activation value in EqualLinear: {activation}Supported ones are: ['fused_lrelu', None]."
                )
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(in_channels) * lr_mul
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(out_channels, in_channels).
            div_(lr_mul))
        if bias:
            self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(out_channels).fill_(
                bias_init_val))
        else:
            self.register_parameter('bias', None)

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.bias is None:
            bias = None
        else:
            bias = self.bias * self.lr_mul
        if self.activation == 'fused_lrelu':
            out = F.linear(x, self.weight * self.scale)
            out = fused_leaky_relu(out, bias)
        else:
            out = F.linear(x, self.weight * self.scale, bias=bias)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}(in_channels={self.in_channels}, out_channels={self.out_channels}, bias={self.bias is not None})'
            )


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_channels': 4, 'out_channels': 4}]

t=9:

7^9=40353607. 40353607-1=40353606=2*(2*9+1)*m²=2*19*m²=38m². 40353606/38=1,062,042.26... Not integer.

This pattern continues without yielding integer m². Therefore, only t=1, corresponding to p=3, gives a valid solution where m is integer.

Alternatively, maybe check if there's a mathematical reason why no other solutions exist.

Looking at the equation 7^{t} =2p m² +1, where p=2t+1.

If p is a prime, then p=2t+1 must be prime.

Suppose t>1. Then 7^t ≡1 mod p. Therefore, the order of 7 modulo p divides t and p-1=2t. The multiplicative order of 7 modulo p must divide gcd(t,2t)=t. So the order of 7 modulo p divides t. But 7^{t}≡1 mod p, as per the equation. Therefore, the order of 7 modulo p divides t. Also, since order divides p-1=2t, we have that the order of 7 modulo p divides gcd(t,2t)=t. Therefore, the order is a divisor of t. Let d be the order of 7 modulo p. Then d divides t. But from Fermat's Little Theorem, 7^{p-1}≡1 modp, so d divides p-1=2t. Since d divides t and d divides 2t, d divides t.

But then, 7^{t}≡1 modp, which we already know because 7^{t}=2p m² +1⇒7^{t}≡1 modp. So this doesn't give new info.

Alternatively, if we write 7^{t} ≡1 modp, then since the order of 7 modulo p is d, then d divides t. Also, d divides p-1=2t. Therefore, d divides gcd(t,2t)=t. Therefore, d divides t and d divides 2t, so d divides t, which we knew. Hence, the order d divides t, and d divides 2t, so d divides t.

But since p is a prime, and p divides 7^{t}-1, then 7^{t}≡1 modp. So 7^{d}≡1 modp, which implies that d divides t, but since d is the order, it's the minimal such exponent. Therefore, unless d=1, which would require 7≡1 modp, which would mean p divides 7-1=6. So p divides 6. The primes dividing 6 are 2 and 3. For p=2, but p=2, we saw the expression is (7^{1}-1)/2=6/2=3, not a square. For p=3, we have a solution. So p=3 is the only prime where d=1.

For other primes, d>1. Therefore, for primes p=2t+1 where d>1, and d divides t, which means that t must be a multiple of d. This might impose restrictions on possible t.

Alternatively, for primes p=2t+1>3, we have 7^{t} ≡1 modp. Let me check for p=5 (t=2). 7^2=49≡4 mod5. 4≡4≠1 mod5. So p=5 is out. Wait, but earlier calculation with p=5 suggested that even though there is a solution to the Pell equation, but in reality, p=5 does not satisfy 7^{t}≡1 modp. Wait, but here, for p=5, t=2, 7^2=49≡4 mod5≠1 mod5. Hence, p=5 cannot satisfy 7^{t}≡1 modp, hence cannot be a solution. But why does it solve the Pell equation? It's because the Pell equation is separate from the modular condition. So even though the Pell equation may have solutions, the actual congruence 7^{t}≡1 modp is not satisfied unless p=3.

Similarly, check p=7 (t=3), but p=7 is a divisor of 7, so 7^{3}=0 mod7≡0, so 0-1=-1≡6 mod7. So (7^{6}-1)/7 is not integer. Therefore, p=7 is invalid.

Wait, but p=3: t=1, 7^1≡7 mod3≡1 mod3, which is correct. Hence, the only prime for which 7^{t}≡1 modp is p=3. For other primes, 7^{t}≡ some other value modulo p.

Therefore, unless p=3, the congruence 7^{t}≡1 modp does not hold. Hence, equation 7^{t} =2p m² +1 cannot hold for any prime p≠3, since 7^{t}≡1 modp is necessary for the right-hand side 2p m² +1≡0 +1≡1 modp, which requires 7^{t}≡1 modp. But only p=3 satisfies that.

Hence, p=3 is the only prime where the equation holds. Therefore, the only solution is p=3.

**Final Answer**
The only prime is \boxed{3}.
</think>To find all primes \( p \) such that \(\left( \frac{7^{p-1} - 1}{p} \right)\) is a perfect square, we will systematically analyze the problem using mathematical tools and properties.

1. **Initial Checks for Small Primes:**

   - For \( p = 2 \):
     \[
     \frac{7^{2-1} - 1}{2} = \frac{7 - 1}{2} = 3 \quad (\text{not a perfect square})
     \]

   - For \( p = 3 \):
     \[
     \frac{7^{3-1} - 1}{3} = \frac{49 - 1}{3} = 16 \quad (\text{perfect square, } 4^2)
     \]

   - For \( p = 5 \):
     \[
     \frac{7^{5-1} - 1}{5} = \frac{2401 - 1}{5} = 480 \quad (\text{not a perfect square})
     \]

   - For \( p = 7 \):
     \[
     \frac{7^{7-1} - 1}{7} = \frac{117649 - 1}{7} = 16806 \quad (\text{not a perfect square})
     \]

2. **General Analysis Using Fermat's Little Theorem:**

   By Fermat's Little Theorem, for any prime \( p \) not dividing 7:
   \[
   7^{p-1} \equiv 1 \mod p
   \]
   Thus, we can write:
   \[
   7^{p-1} = kp + 1
   \]
   for some integer \( k \).

3. **Perfect Square Condition:**

   We need:
   \[
   \frac{7^{p-1} - 1}{p} = k
   \]
   to be a perfect square. Hence:
   \[
   k = m^2 \quad \text{for some integer } m.
   \]
   This gives:
   \[
   7^{p-1} - 1 = p m^2
   \]

4. **Modulo 4 Analysis:**

   Consider \( p \) modulo 4. Since \( p \) is an odd prime, \( p \equiv 1 \mod 4 \) or \( p \equiv 3 \mod 4 \).

   - If \( p \equiv 1 \mod 4 \):
     \[
     7^{p-1} \equiv 1 \mod 4
     \]
     Since \( 7 \equiv 3 \mod<#sep#>);

    for (auto &p: props) {

        int v;

        cin >> v;

        p.vertices.resize(v);

        for (auto& vrt: p.vertices) {

            cin >> vrt.first >> vrt.second;

        }

        // compute area:

        double area =0.0 ;

        for (int i =0; i <v; ++i) {

            auto a = p.vertices[i];

            auto b = p.vertices[ (i+1) %v ];

            area += a.first * b.second;

            area -= b.first * a.first;

        }

        area = 0.5 * fabs(area);

        p.area = area;

    }

    int q;

    cin >> q;

    while (q--){

        double xc, yc, r;

        cin >> xc >> yc >> r;

        double r_sq = r * r;

        vector<int> ids;

        double total =0.0;

        for (int i=0; i< props.size(); ++i){

            const auto& p = props[i];

            bool intersect = false;

            // Vertices check:

            for (const auto& v : p.vertices){

                double dx = v.first - xc;

                double dy = v.second - yc;

                double dsq = dx*dx + dy*dy;

                if (dsq <= r_sq) { 

                    intersect = true;

                    break;

                }

            }

            if (intersect){

                total += p.area;

                ids.push_back(i);

                continue;

            }

            // Edge check:

            // Check each edge (p's edges):

            const int v = p.vertices.size();

            for (int j=0; j < v; j++){

                auto& pntA = p.vertices[j]; 

                auto& pntB = p.vertices[ (j+1) %v ];

                double ax = pntA.first, ay = pntA.second;

                double bx = pntB.first, by = pntB.second;

                double dx_b_a = bx - ax; 

                double dy_b_a = by - ay;

                double length_sq = dx_b_a*dx_b_a + dy_b_a * dy_b_a;

                if (length_sq < 1e-40) continue; // no edge.

                // Compute vector from A to C:

                double dCPx = xc - ax;

                double dCPy = yc - ay;

                double dot = dCPx * dx_b_a + dCPy * dy_b_a;

                double t_ratio = dot / length_sq;

                if ( t_ratio <=0 || t_ratio >=1 ){

                    // closest is endpoints which are not good.

                    continue;

                }

                // compute cross product:

                double cross = dCPx * dy_b_a - dCPy * dx_b_a;

                double numerator_cross = cross * cross;

                double dist_sq = numerator_cross / length_sq;

                if (dist_sq <= r_sq + 1e-8) {

                    // intersection found.

                    intersect = true;

                    break;

                }

            }

            if (intersect){

                total += p.area;

                ids.push_back(i);

            }

        }// end of per property scan for the query.

        // Output section:

        cout.precision(6);

        cout << fixed << total << ' ' << ids.size();

        for (int id: ids) {

            cout << ' ' << id;

        }

        cout << endl;

    }

    return 0;

}

import torch
import warnings
from typing import Optional
from typing import Union
from typing import Any
from typing import Callable
from typing import Tuple
import torch.nn
from torch.nn.modules.loss import _Loss
from enum import Enum
import collections.abc


def issequenceiterable(obj: 'Any') ->bool:
    """
    Determine if the object is an iterable sequence and is not a string.
    """
    if torch.is_tensor(obj):
        return int(obj.dim()) > 0
    return isinstance(obj, collections.abc.Iterable) and not isinstance(obj,
        str)


def ensure_tuple(vals: 'Any') ->Tuple[Any, ...]:
    """
    Returns a tuple of `vals`.
    """
    if not issequenceiterable(vals):
        vals = vals,
    return tuple(vals)


def ensure_tuple_size(tup: 'Any', dim: 'int', pad_val: 'Any'=0) ->Tuple[Any,
    ...]:
    """
    Returns a copy of `tup` with `dim` values by either shortened or padded with `pad_val` as necessary.
    """
    tup = ensure_tuple(tup) + (pad_val,) * dim
    return tuple(tup[:dim])


def one_hot(labels: 'torch.Tensor', num_classes: 'int', dtype:
    'torch.dtype'=torch.float, dim: 'int'=1) ->torch.Tensor:
    """
    For a tensor `labels` of dimensions B1[spatial_dims], return a tensor of dimensions `BN[spatial_dims]`
    for `num_classes` N number of classes.

    Example:

        For every value v = labels[b,1,h,w], the value in the result at [b,v,h,w] will be 1 and all others 0.
        Note that this will include the background label, thus a binary mask should be treated as having 2 classes.
    """
    assert labels.dim() > 0, 'labels should have dim of 1 or more.'
    if labels.ndim < dim + 1:
        shape = ensure_tuple_size(labels.shape, dim + 1, 1)
        labels = labels.reshape(*shape)
    sh = list(labels.shape)
    assert sh[dim
        ] == 1, 'labels should have a channel with length equals to one.'
    sh[dim] = num_classes
    o = torch.zeros(size=sh, dtype=dtype, device=labels.device)
    labels = o.scatter_(dim=dim, index=labels.long(), value=1)
    return labels


class LossReduction(Enum):
    """
    See also:
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.dice.DiceLoss`
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.dice.GeneralizedDiceLoss`
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.focal_loss.FocalLoss`
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.tversky.TverskyLoss`
    """
    NONE = 'none'
    MEAN = 'mean'
    SUM = 'sum'


class DiceLoss(_Loss):
    """
    Compute average Dice loss between two tensors. It can support both multi-classes and multi-labels tasks.
    Input logits `input` (BNHW[D] where N is number of classes) is compared with ground truth `target` (BNHW[D]).
    Axis N of `input` is expected to have logit predictions for each class rather than being image channels,
    while the same axis of `target` can be 1 or N (one-hot format). The `smooth_nr` and `smooth_dr` parameters are
    values added to the intersection and union components of the inter-over-union calculation to smooth results
    respectively, these values should be small. The `include_background` class attribute can be set to False for
    an instance of DiceLoss to exclude the first category (channel index 0) which is by convention assumed to be
    background. If the non-background segmentations are small compared to the total image size they can get
    overwhelmed by the signal from the background so excluding it in such cases helps convergence.

    Milletari, F. et. al. (2016) V-Net: Fully Convolutional Neural Networks forVolumetric Medical Image Segmentation, 3DV, 2016.

    """

    def __init__(self, include_background: 'bool'=True, to_onehot_y: 'bool'
        =False, sigmoid: 'bool'=False, softmax: 'bool'=False, other_act:
        'Optional[Callable]'=None, squared_pred: 'bool'=False, jaccard:
        'bool'=False, reduction: 'Union[LossReduction, str]'=LossReduction.
        MEAN, smooth_nr: 'float'=1e-05, smooth_dr: 'float'=1e-05, batch:
        'bool'=False) ->None:
        """
        Args:
            include_background: if False channel index 0 (background category) is excluded from the calculation.
            to_onehot_y: whether to convert `y` into the one-hot format. Defaults to False.
            sigmoid: if True, apply a sigmoid function to the prediction.
            softmax: if True, apply a softmax function to the prediction.
            other_act: if don't want to use `sigmoid` or `softmax`, use other callable function to execute
                other activation layers, Defaults to ``None``. for example:
                `other_act = torch.tanh`.
            squared_pred: use squared versions of targets and predictions in the denominator or not.
            jaccard: compute Jaccard Index (soft IoU) instead of dice or not.
            reduction: {``"none"``, ``"mean"``, ``"sum"``}
                Specifies the reduction to apply to the output. Defaults to ``"mean"``.

                - ``"none"``: no reduction will be applied.
                - ``"mean"``: the sum of the output will be divided by the number of elements in the output.
                - ``"sum"``: the output will be summed.

            smooth_nr: a small constant added to the numerator to avoid zero.
            smooth_dr: a small constant added to the denominator to avoid nan.
            batch: whether to sum the intersection and union areas over the batch dimension before the dividing.
                Defaults to False, a Dice loss value is computed independently from each item in the batch
                before any `reduction`.

        Raises:
            TypeError: When ``other_act`` is not an ``Optional[Callable]``.
            ValueError: When more than 1 of [``sigmoid=True``, ``softmax=True``, ``other_act is not None``].
                Incompatible values.

        """
        super().__init__(reduction=LossReduction(reduction).value)
        if other_act is not None and not callable(other_act):
            raise TypeError(
                f'other_act must be None or callable but is {type(other_act).__name__}.'
                )
        if int(sigmoid) + int(softmax) + int(other_act is not None) > 1:
            raise ValueError(
                'Incompatible values: more than 1 of [sigmoid=True, softmax=True, other_act is not None].'
                )
        self.include_background = include_background
        self.to_onehot_y = to_onehot_y
        self.sigmoid = sigmoid
        self.softmax = softmax
        self.other_act = other_act
        self.squared_pred = squared_pred
        self.jaccard = jaccard
        self.smooth_nr = float(smooth_nr)
        self.smooth_dr = float(smooth_dr)
        self.batch = batch

    def forward(self, input: 'torch.Tensor', target: 'torch.Tensor'
        ) ->torch.Tensor:
        """
        Args:
            input: the shape should be BNH[WD].
            target: the shape should be BNH[WD].

        Raises:
            ValueError: When ``self.reduction`` is not one of ["mean", "sum", "none"].

        """
        if self.sigmoid:
            input = torch.sigmoid(input)
        n_pred_ch = input.shape[1]
        if self.softmax:
            if n_pred_ch == 1:
                warnings.warn(
                    'single channel prediction, `softmax=True` ignored.')
            else:
                input = torch.softmax(input, 1)
        if self.other_act is not None:
            input = self.other_act(input)
        if self.to_onehot_y:
            if n_pred_ch == 1:
                warnings.warn(
                    'single channel prediction, `to_onehot_y=True` ignored.')
            else:
                target = one_hot(target, num_classes=n_pred_ch)
        if not self.include_background:
            if n_pred_ch == 1:
                warnings.warn(
                    'single channel prediction, `include_background=False` ignored.'
                    )
            else:
                target = target[:, 1:]
                input = input[:, 1:]
        assert target.shape == input.shape, f'ground truth has differing shape ({target.shape}) from input ({input.shape})'
        reduce_axis = list(range(2<#sep#>:
   \[
   7^{p-1} - 1 = p m^2
   \]

4. **Modulo 4 Analysis:**

   Consider \( p \) modulo 4. Since \( p \) is an odd prime, \( p \equiv 1 \mod 4 \) or \( p \equiv 3 \mod 4 \).

   - If \( p \equiv 1 \mod 4 \):
     \[
     7^{p-1} \equiv 1 \mod 4
     \]
     Since \( 7 \equiv 3 \mod 4 \), we have:
     \[
     7^{p-1} \equiv 3^{p-1} \mod 4
     \]
     Since \( p-1 \) is even:
     \[
     3^{p-1} \equiv 1 \mod 4
     \]
     Therefore:
     \[
     7^{p-1} \equiv 1 \mod 4
     \]
     This implies:
     \[
     7^{p-1} - 1 \equiv 0 \mod 4
     \]
     Hence, \( p m^2 \equiv 0 \mod 4 \). Since \( p \equiv 1 \mod 4 \), \( m^2 \equiv 0 \mod 4 \), so \( m \) must be even.

   - If \( p \equiv 3 \mod 4 \):
     \[
     7^{p-1} \equiv 1 \mod 4
     \]
     Similarly, \( 3^{p-1} \equiv 1 \mod 4 \), so:
     \[
     7^{p-1} \equiv 1 \mod 4
     \]
     This again implies:
     \[
     7^{p-1} - 1 \equiv 0 \mod 4
     \]
     Hence, \( p m^2 \equiv 0 \mod 4 \). Since \( p \equiv 3 \mod 4 \), \( m^2 \equiv 0 \mod 4 \), so \( m \) must be even.

5. **Specific Case Analysis:**

   We need to check if there are any other primes \( p \) beyond \( p = 3 \) that satisfy the equation:
   \[
   7^{(p-1)/2} = 2k^2 - 1
   \]
   and
   \[
   7^{(p-1)/2} - 1 = 2p m^2
   \]
   for some integers \( k \) and \( m \).

   - For \( p = 3 \):
     \[
     7^{(3-1)/2} = 7^1 = 7
     \]
     \[
     7 = 2k^2 - 1 \implies k^2 = 4 \implies k = 2
     \]
     \[
     \frac{7^2 - 1}{3} = 16 \quad (\text{perfect square})
     \]

   - For \( p > 3 \), we need to check if \( 7^{(p-1)/2} \equiv 1 \mod p \) holds. By Fermat's Little Theorem, this is only satisfied for \( p = 3 \).

Therefore, the only prime \( p \) such that \(\left( \frac{7^{p-1} - 1}{p} \right)\) is a perfect square is:

        reduce_axis = list(range(2, len(input.shape)))
        if self.batch:

        intersection = torch.sum(target * input, dim=reduce_axis)
        if self.squared_pred:

            input = torch.pow(input, 2)
        ground_o = torch.sum(target, dim=reduce_axis)
        pred_o = torch.sum(input, dim=reduce_axis)
        denominator = ground_o + pred_o
        if self.jaccard:
            denominator = 2.0 * (denominator - intersection)
        f: 'torch.Tensor' = 1.0 - (2.0 * intersection + self.smooth_nr) / (
            denominator + self.smooth_dr)
        if self.reduction == LossReduction.MEAN.value:
            f = torch.mean(f)
        elif self.reduction == LossReduction.SUM.value:
            f = torch.sum(f)
        elif self.reduction == LossReduction.NONE.value:
            pass
        else:
            raise ValueError(
                f'Unsupported reduction: {self.reduction}, available options are ["mean", "sum", "none"].'
                )
        return f

Now,<#sep#>human: Some days ago, WJMZBMR learned how to answer the query "how many times does a string x occur in a string s" quickly by preprocessing the string s. But now he wants to make it harder.

So he wants to ask "how many consecutive substrings of s are cyclical isomorphic to a given string x". You are given string s and n strings xi, for each string xi find, how many consecutive substrings of s are cyclical isomorphic to xi.

Two strings are called cyclical isomorphic if one can rotate one string to get the other one. 'Rotate' here means 'to take some consecutive chars (maybe none) from the beginning of a string and put them back at the end of the string in the same order'. For example, string "abcde" can be rotated to string "deabc". We can take characters "abc" from the beginning and put them at the end of "de".
Input

The first line contains a non-empty string s. The length of string s is not greater than 106 characters.

The second line contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 105) — the number of queries. Then n lines follow: the i-th line contains the string xi — the string for the i-th query. The total length of xi is less than or equal to 106 characters.

In this problem, strings only consist of lowercase English letters.

Output

For each query xi print a single integer that shows how many consecutive substrings of s are cyclical isomorphic to xi. Print the answers to the queries in the order they are given in the input.

Examples

Input

baabaabaaa
5
a
ba
baa
aabaa
aaba


Output

7
5
7
3
5


Input

aabbaa
3
aa
aabb
abba


Output

2
3
3

gpt: <think> Okay, so I need to solve this problem where for each query string x, I have to find how many substrings of s are cyclical isomorphic to x. Cyclical isomorphic means that one can be rotated into the other. Hmm, right, rotation here is like shifting some prefix to the end. Like "abcde" can become "deabc" by moving the first three letters to the end.

So, the key is that two strings are cyclically isomorphic if one is a rotation of the other. To find the number of substrings of s that are rotations of x, I need a way to represent all the rotations of x and count how many times any of those rotations appear as substrings in s. But doing this naively for each query would be way too slow, especially since the queries can be up to 1e5 and the strings can be large.

First, I need to think about an efficient way to handle this. The usual approach for substring queries is to use a suffix array or maybe the Knuth-Morris-Pratt (KMP) algorithm, but since rotations are involved, maybe a different approach is better.

Wait, rotations of a string x form a set of strings where each rotation is equivalent under rotation. For a given x of length m, each of its rotations is another string of length m. So for each x, there are m possible rotations, but maybe some of them are the same. For example, if x is "aaa", all rotations are the same. 

So for each query xi, we need to consider all its rotations and check how many times any of those rotations appear as substrings in s. But how to do this efficiently?

Alternatively, maybe there's a canonical form for cyclic rotations, so that two strings are rotationally isomorphic if and only if their canonical forms are the same. Then, for a given x, we can compute its canonical form, and then for the substrings of s, compute their canonical forms, and count how many of them equal the canonical form of the query.

If that's possible, then the problem reduces to:

1. Preprocessing the string s such that for every possible substring s' of s, we can quickly compute its canonical form, and then tally the counts for each canonical form.

Wait, but s can be up to 1e6 characters long, so doing this for every substring is impossible since there are O(n^2) substrings. So that's way too big even for n=1e6.

Hmm, so we can't store all substrings. That approach is out.

Alternatively, the canonical form might be such that we can represent it in a hash-like manner. For example, the minimal lex rotation, like the lex smallest rotation. Then two strings are rotations of each other iff their minimal rotations are equal. 

Yes, that's a common approach. For example, when you want to check if two strings are rotations of each other, you can concatenate the string to itself and check if the other is a substring (but this might not consider all rotations). Wait, but to find the minimal lexicographical rotation is a standard way to canonicalize the rotation.

So here's an idea: To check if a target string (like xi) has any rotation present in s as a substring, we can represent the target's canonical form (its minimal rotated form), and then look for how many substrings in s have that canonical form.

Wait, but the problem requires to count all substrings of s of length m (where m is the length of the query) that are rotations of the query. The query's length is fixed, so for each query xi (length m), we need to count all m-length substrings of s which are rotations of xi. 

So the approach would be:

For each query xi of length m, first compute all possible rotations of xi, but compute a canonical form for xi (such that any rotation of xi would have the same canonical form as xi). Then for every m-length substring of s, also compute its canonical form, and then when a query comes in, it's just comparing the canonical form of the query with all m-length substrings of s's canonical forms, and counting how many matches there are.

But how do we compute canonical forms efficiently for all possible m-length substrings?

Because for every substring of s of length m, to compute its canonical form, and then track frequencies. But since m can be up to 1e6, and there are 1e5 queries with varying m, this could be tricky.

Alternatively, perhaps instead, we can generate for each possible m and each substring, but that's going to be too slow. So I need a better idea.

Hmm. Let's think of the canonical form as the minimal rotation lex, and compute for each occurrence of a substring of hte required length their lex minimal rotations' hashes, so that when a query's hash is known, we can quickly check how many substrings have the same hash.

Wait but how?

Let me think again. If two strings are rotations, then their minimal rotations are the same, so the number of substrings that are rotationally isomorphic to xi is equal to the number of substrings of s equal to the minimal rotation of xi (or any other canonical form, as long as it is the same for all rotations of xi). Therefore, to compute for the query xi, the number would be the count of occurrences of xi's canonical form in s (as a substring in any position with length m).

So the plan is:

1. For each query xi, compute its canonical form (e.g., the minimal rotation lex or any unique hash).

2. Then count how many times that canonical form appears in s as a substring of length m.

Thus the problem reduces to, for each query xi, how to compute its canonical form, and then count the occurrences of that form in s's substrings of the same length.

The key steps here are:

- How to compute the minimal rotation lex form for a string, efficiently.

- For a given length m, how to quickly count the number of substrings of s of length m with a certain canonical form (or at least, with a particular hash that we can compute on the fly for the canonical form).

But even the second step could be challenging. Let's tackle the first part first.

def make_resample_kernel(k):
    """Make resampling kernel for UpFirDn.

    Args:
        k (list[int]): A list indicating the 1D resample kernel magnitude.

    Returns:
        Tensor: 2D resampled kernel.
    """
    k = torch.tensor(k, dtype=torch.float32)
    if k.ndim == 1:
        k = k[None, :] * k[:, None]
    k /= k.sum()
    return k


def upfirdn2d_native(input, kernel, up_x, up_y, down_x, down_y, pad_x0,
    pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1):
    _, channel, in_h, in_w = input.shape
    input = input.reshape(-1, in_h, in_w, 1)
    _, in_h, in_w, minor = input.shape
    kernel_h, kernel_w = kernel.shape
    out = input.view(-1, in_h, 1, in_w, 1, minor)
    out = F.pad(out, [0, 0, 0, up_x - 1, 0, 0, 0, up_y - 1])
    out = out.view(-1, in_h * up_y, in_w * up_x, minor)
    out = F.pad(out, [0, 0, max(pad_x0, 0), max(pad_x1, 0), max(pad_y0, 0),
        max(pad_y1, 0)])
    out = out[:, max(-pad_y0, 0):out.shape[1] - max(-pad_y1, 0), max(-
        pad_x0, 0):out.shape[2] - max(-pad_x1, 0), :]
    out = out.permute(0, 3, 1, 2)
    out = out.reshape([-1, 1, in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1, in_w * up_x +
        pad_x0 + pad_x1])
    w = torch.flip(kernel, [0, 1]).view(1, 1, kernel_h, kernel_w)
    out = F.conv2d(out, w)
    out = out.reshape(-1, minor, in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1 - kernel_h +
        1, in_w * up_x + pad_x0 + pad_x1 - kernel_w + 1)
    out = out.permute(0, 2, 3, 1)
    out = out[:, ::down_y, ::down_x, :]
    out_h = (in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1 - kernel_h) // down_y + 1
    out_w = (in_w * up_x + pad_x0 + pad_x1 - kernel_w) // down_x + 1
    return out.view(-1, channel, out_h, out_w)


def upfirdn2d(input, kernel, up=1, down=1, pad=(0, 0)):
    if input.device.type == 'cpu':
        out = upfirdn2d_native(input, kernel, up, up, down, down, pad[0],
            pad[1], pad[0], pad[1])
    else:
        out = UpFirDn2d.apply(input, kernel, (up, up), (down, down), (pad[0
            ], pad[1], pad[0], pad[1]))
    return out


def fused_leaky_relu(input, bias, negative_slope=0.2, scale=2 ** 0.5):
    return FusedLeakyReLUFunction.apply(input, bias, negative_slope, scale)


class UpFirDn2dBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, kernel, grad_kernel, up, down, pad, g_pad,
        in_size, out_size):
        up_x, up_y = up
        down_x, down_y = down
        g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1 = g_pad
        grad_output = grad_output.reshape(-1, out_size[0], out_size[1], 1)
        grad_input = upfirdn2d_ext.upfirdn2d(grad_output, grad_kernel,
            down_x, down_y, up_x, up_y, g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1)
        grad_input = grad_input.view(in_size[0], in_size[1], in_size[2],
            in_size[3])
        ctx.save_for_backward(kernel)
        pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1 = pad
        ctx.up_x = up_x
        ctx.up_y = up_y
        ctx.down_x = down_x
        ctx.down_y = down_y
        ctx.pad_x0 = pad_x0
        ctx.pad_x1 = pad_x1
        ctx.pad_y0 = pad_y0
        ctx.pad_y1 = pad_y1
        ctx.in_size = in_size
        ctx.out_size = out_size
        return grad_input

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input):
        kernel, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_input = gradgrad_input.reshape(-1, ctx.in_size[2], ctx.
            in_size[3], 1)
        gradgrad_out = upfirdn2d_ext.upfirdn2d(gradgrad_input, kernel, ctx.
            up_x, ctx.up_y, ctx.down_x, ctx.down_y, ctx.pad_x0, ctx.pad_x1,
            ctx.pad_y0, ctx.pad_y1)
        gradgrad_out = gradgrad_out.view(ctx.in_size[0], ctx.in_size[1],
            ctx.out_size[0], ctx.out_size[1])
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None, None, None, None, None, None


class UpFirDn2d(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, kernel, up, down, pad):
        up_x, up_y = up
        down_x, down_y = down
        pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1 = pad
        kernel_h, kernel_w = kernel.shape
        _batch, channel, in_h, in_w = input.shape
        ctx.in_size = input.shape
        input = input.reshape(-1, in_h, in_w, 1)
        ctx.save_for_backward(kernel, torch.flip(kernel, [0, 1]))
        out_h = (in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1 - kernel_h) // down_y + 1
        out_w = (in_w * up_x + pad_x0 + pad_x1 - kernel_w) // down_x + 1
        ctx.out_size = out_h, out_w
        ctx.up = up_x, up_y
        ctx.down = down_x, down_y
        ctx.pad = pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1
        g_pad_x0 = kernel_w - pad_x0 - 1
        g_pad_y0 = kernel_h - pad_y0 - 1
        g_pad_x1 = in_w * up_x - out_w * down_x + pad_x0 - up_x + 1
        g_pad_y1 = in_h * up_y - out_h * down_y + pad_y0 - up_y + 1
        ctx.g_pad = g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1
        out = upfirdn2d_ext.upfirdn2d(input, kernel, up_x, up_y, down_x,
            down_y, pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1)
        out = out.view(-1, channel, out_h, out_w)
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        kernel, grad_kernel = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input = UpFirDn2dBackward.apply(grad_output, kernel,
            grad_kernel, ctx.up, ctx.down, ctx.pad, ctx.g_pad, ctx.in_size,
            ctx.out_size)
        return grad_input, None, None, None, None

</think>To find the number of positive integers not exceeding 2001 that are multiples of 3 or 4 but not 5, we use the principle of inclusion-exclusion.

1. **Count multiples of 3 or 4:**
   - Number of multiples of 3 up to 2001: \(\left\lfloor \frac{2001}{3} \right\rfloor = 667\)
   - Number of multiples of 4 up to 2001: \(\left\lfloor \frac{2001}{4} \right\rfloor = 500\)
   - Number of multiples of both 3 and 4 (LCM of 3 and 4 is 12): \(\left\lfloor \frac{2001}{12} \right\rfloor = 166\)

   Using the principle of inclusion-exclusion, the number of integers up to 2001 that are multiples of 3 or 4 is:
   \[
   667 + 500 - 166 = 1001
   \]

2. **Subtract multiples of 15 or 20 (since these are multiples of 3 or 4 and also 5):**
   - Number of multiples of 15 up to 2001: \(\left\lfloor \frac{2001}{15} \right\rfloor = 133\)
   - Number of multiples of 20 up to 2001: \(\left\lfloor \frac{2001}{20} \right\rfloor = 100\)
   - Number of multiples of both 15 and 20 (LCM of 15 and 20 is 60): \(\left\lfloor \frac{2001}{60} \right\rfloor = 33\)

   Using the principle of inclusion-exclusion, the number of integers up to 2001 that are multiples of 15 or 20 is:
   \[
   133 + 100 - 33 = 200
   \]

3. **Final result:**
   - Subtract the count of multiples of 15 or 20 from the count of multiples of 3 or 4:
   \[
   1001 - 200 = 801
   \]

Thus, the number of positive integers not exceeding 2001 that are multiples of 3 or 4 but not 5 is \(\boxed{801}\).<#sep#> the first part first.

Computing minimal rotation lex form for a string xi:

To find the minimal rotation lex form of a string of length m, the standard approach is to consider all rotations and pick the lex smallest. The classic way is in O(m^2) time, but m can be up to 1e6? No, but since in each query, the xi's total length is up to 1e6, that's manageable.

Wait, but if each query's length is up to 1e6, but the total sum of lengths of xi across queries is 1e6, then the total time across all queries is acceptable. Let's see. The minimal rotation can be done in O(m log m) time using Booth's algorithm (Booth's is for finding all runs, but maybe there's a linear time minimal rotation algorithm). Alternatively, maybe in O(m) time? 

Hmm, Booth's algorithm finds the lex smallest rotation in linear time, right?

Yes, Booth's algorithm can find the minimal lexicographical rotation of a circular string in O(m) time. Oh right! Here's a link for the algorithm. Let me recall: The idea is similar to building a suffix automaton or something else. Let me think.

The algorithm is similar to Floyd's cycle-finding algorithm but for rotations. Let me see... Booth's algorithm is used to find the minimal rotation in O(m) time. So for each query xi, we can compute its minimal rotation in O(m) time. So since the total sum of query lengths is 1e6, this is manageable.

Once we have that minimal form, we need to find how many times that form appears as a substring in s.

Wait, but the minimal rotation is just one representative of the rotation group, so any rotation of xi is similar to this form. So, when looking in s for any substring equal to xi's minimal rotation, we can use standard substring search methods.

So the problem becomes equivalent to for each query, we need to find the number of occurrences of the minimal rotation (of the query) in s as a substring, but all substrings of exactly length m.

So to do this efficiently, for each possible length m, we can precompute a hash table (or a structure) that allows counting how many occurrences each possible substring of length m exists in s.

However, precomputing this for all possible m is difficult. Because s is up to 1e6 characters, and m can be up to 1e6, that would take O(1e12) storage, which is not feasible. 

Alternatively, perhaps we can, for each query, process the query's minimal rotation string and then perform a search for it in s, while considering the exact length.

Wait, but doing that would require for each query to perform a pattern search on s. The worst case here could be bad. However, using the KMP algorithm for each query would take O(m + len_s) time for each. Assuming the total of m across queries is 1e6, perhaps this is manageable. Let's see.

Wait, if the query length is m, the KMP for each query is O(m + N), where N is the length of s (assuming s is up to 1e6). So for n=1e5 queries with each m=1e4, the total time would be 1e5*(1e4+1e6) = 1e11 operations which is way too slow.

That's not going to work. Hmm. Need a better way.

So perhaps pre-processing s in such a way that we can answer, for each substring length m, a hash of the canonical form for all its substrings of length m.

Wait, here is an idea. Let me think:

For a fixed desired length m, all we need is, in s, how many substrings of length m have a minimal lex rotation equal to the minimal lex rotation of x.

To compute this, it may be necessary for all substrings of s of length m, to compute their minimal rotation lex string, and count how often each such string occurs, and then for each query, compute the minimal rotation of xi and look up its count.

However, precomputing for all possible m and storing a frequency table for all possible minimal rotation strings is impossible, because m can be up to 1e6 and the number of possible strings is astronomical.

Hmm, so perhaps we can compute the minimal rotation's hash (using some hash) for all possible substrings of s of varying lengths, but storing all that is not possible.

class UpFirDnUpsample(nn.Module):
    """Upsample, FIR filter, and downsample (upsampole version).

    References:
    1. https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.signal.upfirdn.html  # noqa: E501
    2. http://www.ece.northwestern.edu/local-apps/matlabhelp/toolbox/signal/upfirdn.html  # noqa: E501

    Args:
        resample_kernel (list[int]): A list indicating the 1D resample kernel
            magnitude.
        factor (int): Upsampling scale factor. Default: 2.
    """

    def __init__(self, resample_kernel, factor=2):
        super(UpFirDnUpsample, self).__init__()
        self.kernel = make_resample_kernel(resample_kernel) * factor ** 2
        self.factor = factor
        pad = self.kernel.shape[0] - factor
        self.pad = (pad + 1) // 2 + factor - 1, pad // 2

    def forward(self, x):
        out = upfirdn2d(x, self.kernel.type_as(x), up=self.factor, down=1,
            pad=self.pad)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return f'{self.__class__.__name__}(factor={self.factor})'


class UpFirDnSmooth(nn.Module):
    """Upsample, FIR filter, and downsample (smooth version).

    Args:
        resample_kernel (list[int]): A list indicating the 1D resample kernel
            magnitude.
        upsample_factor (int): Upsampling scale factor. Default: 1.
        downsample_factor (int): Downsampling scale factor. Default: 1.
        kernel_size (int): Kernel size: Deafult: 1.
    """

    def __init__(self, resample_kernel, upsample_factor=1,
        downsample_factor=1, kernel_size=1):
        super(UpFirDnSmooth, self).__init__()
        self.upsample_factor = upsample_factor
        self.downsample_factor = downsample_factor
        self.kernel = make_resample_kernel(resample_kernel)
        if upsample_factor > 1:
            self.kernel = self.kernel * upsample_factor ** 2
        if upsample_factor > 1:
            pad = self.kernel.shape[0] - upsample_factor - (kernel_size - 1)
            self.pad = (pad + 1) // 2 + upsample_factor - 1, pad // 2 + 1
        elif downsample_factor > 1:
            pad = self.kernel.shape[0] - downsample_factor + (kernel_size - 1)
            self.pad = (pad + 1) // 2, pad // 2
        else:
            raise NotImplementedError

    def forward(self, x):
        out = upfirdn2d(x, self.kernel.type_as(x), up=1, down=1, pad=self.pad)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}(upsample_factor={self.upsample_factor}, downsample_factor={self.downsample_factor})'
            )


class FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, out, negative_slope, scale):
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        empty = grad_output.new_empty(0)
        grad_input = fused_act_ext.fused_bias_act(grad_output, empty, out, 
            3, 1, negative_slope, scale)
        dim = [0]
        if grad_input.ndim > 2:
            dim += list(range(2, grad_input.ndim))
        grad_bias = grad_input.sum(dim).detach()
        return grad_input, grad_bias

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input, gradgrad_bias):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_out = fused_act_ext.fused_bias_act(gradgrad_input,
            gradgrad_bias, out, 3, 1, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None


class FusedLeakyReLUFunction(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, bias, negative_slope, scale):
        empty = input.new_empty(0)
        out = fused_act_ext.fused_bias_act(input, bias, empty, 3, 0,
            negative_slope, scale)
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input, grad_bias = FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward.apply(
            grad_output, out, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return grad_input, grad_bias, None, None


class EqualLinear(nn.Module):
    """Equalized Linear as StyleGAN2.

    Args:
        in_channels (int): Size of each sample.
        out_channels (int): Size of each output sample.
        bias (bool): If set to ``False``, the layer will not learn an additive
            bias. Default: ``True``.
        bias_init_val (float): Bias initialized value. Default: 0.
        lr_mul (float): Learning rate multiplier. Default: 1.
        activation (None | str): The activation after ``linear`` operation.
            Supported: 'fused_lrelu', None. Default: None.
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, bias=True, bias_init_val=
        0, lr_mul=1, activation=None):
        super(EqualLinear, self).__init__()
        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.out_channels = out_channels
        self.lr_mul = lr_mul
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation not in ['fused_lrelu', None]:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Wrong activation value in EqualLinear: {activation}Supported ones are: ['fused_lrelu', None]."
                )
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(in_channels) * lr_mul
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(out_channels, in_channels).
            div_(lr_mul))
        if bias:
            self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(out_channels).fill_(
                bias_init_val))
        else:
            self.register_parameter('bias', None)

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.bias is None:
            bias = None
        else:
            bias = self.bias * self.lr_mul
        if self.activation == 'fused_lrelu':
            out = F.linear(x, self.weight * self.scale)
            out = fused_leaky_relu(out, bias)
        else:
            out = F.linear(x, self.weight * self.scale, bias=bias)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}(in_channels={self.in_channels}, out_channels={self.out_channels}, bias={self.bias is not None})'
            )


class ModulatedConv2d(nn.Module):
    """Modulated Conv2d used in StyleGAN2.

    There is no bias in ModulatedConv2d.

    Args:
        in_channels (int): Channel number of the input.
        out_channels (int): Channel number of the output.
        kernel_size (int): Size of the convolving kernel.
        num_style_feat (int): Channel number of style features.
        demodulate (bool): Whether to demodulate in the conv layer.
            Default: True.
        sample_mode (str | None): Indicating 'upsample', 'downsample' or None.
            Default: None.
        resample_kernel (list[int]): A list indicating the 1D resample kernel
            magnitude. Default: (1, 3, 3, 1).
        eps (float): A value added to the denominator for numerical stability.
            Default: 1e-8.
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size,
        num_style_feat, demodulate=True, sample_mode=None, resample_kernel=
        (1, 3, 3, 1), eps=1e-08):
        super(ModulatedConv2d, self).__init__()
        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.out_channels = out_channels
        self.kernel_size = kernel_size
        self.demodulate = demodulate
        self.sample_mode = sample_mode

  <#sep#> added to the denominator for numerical stability.
            Default: 1e-8.
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size,
        num_style_feat, demodulate=True, sample_mode=None, resample_kernel=
        (1, 3, 3, 1), eps=1e-08):
        super(ModulatedConv2d, self).__init__()
        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.out_channels = out_channels
        self.kernel_size = kernel_size
        self.demodulate = demodulate
        self.sample_mode = sample_mode
        self.eps = eps
        if self.sample_mode == 'upsample':
            self.smooth = UpFirDnSmooth(resample_kernel, upsample_factor=2,
                downsample_factor=1, kernel_size=kernel_size)
        elif self.sample_mode == 'downsample':
            self.smooth = UpFirDnSmooth(resample_kernel, upsample_factor=1,
                downsample_factor=2, kernel_size=kernel_size)
        elif self.sample_mode is None:
            pass
        else:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Wrong sample mode {self.sample_mode}, supported ones are ['upsample', 'downsample', None]."
                )
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(in_channels * kernel_size ** 2)
        self.modulation = EqualLinear(num_style_feat, in_channels, bias=
            True, bias_init_val=1, lr_mul=1, activation=None)
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(1, out_channels, in_channels,
            kernel_size, kernel_size))
        self.padding = kernel_size // 2

    def forward(self, x, style):
        """Forward function.

        Args:
            x (Tensor): Tensor with shape (b, c, h, w).
            style (Tensor): Tensor with shape (b, num_style_feat).

        Returns:
            Tensor: Modulated tensor after convolution.
        """
        b, c, h, w = x.shape
        style = self.modulation(style).view(b, 1, c, 1, 1)
        weight = self.scale * self.weight * style
        if self.demodulate:
            demod = torch.rsqrt(weight.pow(2).sum([2, 3, 4]) + self.eps)
            weight = weight * demod.view(b, self.out_channels, 1, 1, 1)
        weight = weight.view(b * self.out_channels, c, self.kernel_size,
            self.kernel_size)
        if self.sample_mode == 'upsample':
            x = x.view(1, b * c, h, w)
            weight = weight.view(b, self.out_channels, c, self.kernel_size,
                self.kernel_size)
            weight = weight.transpose(1, 2).reshape(b * c, self.
                out_channels, self.kernel_size, self.kernel_size)
            out = F.conv_transpose2d(x, weight, padding=0, stride=2, groups=b)
            out = out.view(b, self.out_channels, *out.shape[2:4])
            out = self.smooth(out)
        elif self.sample_mode == 'downsample':
            x = self.smooth(x)
            x = x.view(1, b * c, *x.shape[2:4])
            out = F.conv2d(x, weight, padding=0, stride=2, groups=b)
            out = out.view(b, self.out_channels, *out.shape[2:4])
        else:
            x = x.view(1, b * c, h, w)
            out = F.conv2d(x, weight, padding=self.padding, groups=b)
            out = out.view(b, self.out_channels, *out.shape[2:4])
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}(in_channels={self.in_channels}, out_channels={self.out_channels}, kernel_size={self.kernel_size}, demodulate={self.demodulate}, sample_mode={self.sample_mode})'
            )


class ToRGB(nn.Module):
    """To RGB from features.

    Args:
        in_channels (int): Channel number of input.
        num_style_feat (int): Channel number of style features.
        upsample (bool): Whether to upsample. Default: True.
        resample_kernel (list[int]): A list indicating the 1D resample kernel
            magnitude. Default: (1, 3, 3, 1).
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels, num_style_feat, upsample=True,
        resample_kernel=(1, 3, 3, 1)):
        super(ToRGB, self).__init__()
        if upsample:
            self.upsample = UpFirDnUpsample(resample_kernel, factor=2)
        else:
            self.upsample = None
        self.modulated_conv = ModulatedConv2d(in_channels, 3, kernel_size=1,
            num_style_feat=num_style_feat, demodulate=False, sample_mode=None)
        self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(1, 3, 1, 1))

    def forward(self, x, style, skip=None):
        """Forward function.

        Args:
            x (Tensor): Feature tensor with shape (b, c, h, w).
            style (Tensor): Tensor with shape (b, num_style_feat).
            skip (Tensor): Base/skip tensor. Default: None.

        Returns:
            Tensor: RGB images.
        """
        out = self.modulated_conv(x, style)
        out = out + self.bias
        if skip is not None:
            if self.upsample:
                skip = self.upsample(skip)
            out = out + skip
        return out


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_channels': 4, 'num_style_feat': 4}]

Here's the Idea:

Precompute for the string s a hash map for every possible length m. For each m length, store a dictionary that maps hash values (computed using rolling hash) to the count of how many times the hash occurs for a substring of length m.

But the problem is storage.

The maximum possible m for any query is 1e6. For each m from 1 to N (where N is length of s), we need to store the count of each hash for m-length substrings.

The number of possible m is up to 1e6, for each m there are (N-m+1) possible substrings. The number of different hash values can be up to (N-m+1), so storing this as a dictionary per m could take O(N^2) space, which is impossible.

Thus, this is not feasible. 

Wait, but what if we do it dynamically? For each query, compute its minimal rotation, then perform a substring search using the rolling hash approach, but only for that query.

Yes, here's the approach:

Precompute prefix hashes and the necessary power values for s.

Then, for each query xi:

1. Compute its minimal rotation string Y, length m.

2. Compute the target hash of Y using the same rolling hash as used in pre-processing s.

3. Iterate over all starting indices i in s where the substring s[i..i+m) is exactly of length m.

4. To compute the hash of s[i..i+m), use the precomputed rolling hashes to get that in O(1) per substring.

5. Count the number of times the target hash is seen (minus any collisions? But perhaps probability is low).

So this process for each query takes O(m) time for minimal rotation, then O(N) for checking all substrings of length m, with O(1) per substring hash check. 

Thus total time is:

sum over queries (O(m_i) + O(N)) ), where m_i is the length of each query. 

Wait, but if the number of queries is 1e5 and N is 1e6, that is the total is 1e5 *1e6 = 1e11 operations. Which is way too big, computationally impossible. 

That’s not going to work for the given constraints.

The suffix automaton can do this. Let me recall how:

The suffix automaton has a size O(N), and each edge corresponds to a set of transitions. 

To count the number of occurrences of a string Y in s, we can traverse the automaton and for each character in Y follow the transitions, and at the end node, the count of occurrences is stored (or can be computed by backtracking). 

In the suffix automaton, each node can hold the number of end positions, which is the number of occurrences of the substring ending at that node. Wait, maybe the count is the number of end positions, which can help compute the occurrences.

   <#sep#>from torch.autograd import Function
import math
import torch
from torch import nn as nn
from torch.nn import functional as F
from torch.nn import init as init
from torchvision.models import vgg as vgg
import torch.utils.data
from torch.utils import data as data
from torch import autograd as autograd


def make_resample_kernel(k):
    """Make resampling kernel for UpFirDn.

    Args:
        k (list[int]): A list indicating the 1D resample kernel magnitude.

    Returns:
        Tensor: 2D resampled kernel.
    """
    k = torch.tensor(k, dtype=torch.float32)
    if k.ndim == 1:
        k = k[None, :] * k[:, None]
    k /= k.sum()
    return k


def upfirdn2d_native(input, kernel, up_x, up_y, down_x, down_y, pad_x0,
    pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1):
    _, channel, in_h, in_w = input.shape
    input = input.reshape(-1, in_h, in_w, 1)
    _, in_h, in_w, minor = input.shape
    kernel_h, kernel_w = kernel.shape
    out = input.view(-1, in_h, 1, in_w, 1, minor)
    out = F.pad(out, [0, 0, 0, up_x - 1, 0, 0, 0, up_y - 1])
    out = out.view(-1, in_h * up_y, in_w * up_x, minor)
    out = F.pad(out, [0, 0, max(pad_x0, 0), max(pad_x1, 0), max(pad_y0, 0),
        max(pad_y1, 0)])
    out = out[:, max(-pad_y0, 0):out.shape[1] - max(-pad_y1, 0), max(-
        pad_x0, 0):out.shape[2] - max(-pad_x1, 0), :]
    out = out.permute(0, 3, 1, 2)
    out = out.reshape([-1, 1, in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1, in_w * up_x +
        pad_x0 + pad_x1])
    w = torch.flip(kernel, [0, 1]).view(1, 1, kernel_h, kernel_w)
    out = F.conv2d(out, w)
    out = out.reshape(-1, minor, in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1 - kernel_h +
        1, in_w * up_x + pad_x0 + pad_x1 - kernel_w + 1)
    out = out.permute(0, 2, 3, 1)
    out = out[:, ::down_y, ::down_x, :]
    out_h = (in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1 - kernel_h) // down_y + 1
    out_w = (in_w * up_x + pad_x0 + pad_x1 - kernel_w) // down_x + 1
    return out.view(-1, channel, out_h, out_w)


def upfirdn2d(input, kernel, up=1, down=1, pad=(0, 0)):
    if input.device.type == 'cpu':
        out = upfirdn2d_native(input, kernel, up, up, down, down, pad[0],
            pad[1], pad[0], pad[1])
    else:
        out = UpFirDn2d.apply(input, kernel, (up, up), (down, down), (pad[0
            ], pad[1], pad[0], pad[1]))
    return out


def fused_leaky_relu(input, bias, negative_slope=0.2, scale=2 ** 0.5):
    return FusedLeakyReLUFunction.apply(input, bias, negative_slope, scale)


class UpFirDn2dBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, kernel, grad_kernel, up, down, pad, g_pad,
        in_size, out_size):
        up_x, up_y = up
        down_x, down_y = down
        g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1 = g_pad
        grad_output = grad_output.reshape(-1, out_size[0], out_size[1], 1)
        grad_input = upfirdn2d_ext.upfirdn2d(grad_output, grad_kernel,
            down_x, down_y, up_x, up_y, g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1)
        grad_input = grad_input.view(in_size[0], in_size[1], in_size[2],
            in_size[3])
        ctx.save_for_backward(kernel)
        pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1 = pad
        ctx.up_x = up_x
        ctx.up_y = up_y
        ctx.down_x = down_x
        ctx.down_y = down_y
        ctx.pad_x0 = pad_x0
        ctx.pad_x1 = pad_x1
        ctx.pad_y0 = pad_y0
        ctx.pad_y1 = pad_y1
        ctx.in_size = in_size
        ctx.out_size = out_size
        return grad_input

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input):
        kernel, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_input = gradgrad_input.reshape(-1, ctx.in_size[2], ctx.
            in_size[3], 1)
        gradgrad_out = upfirdn2d_ext.upfirdn2d(gradgrad_input, kernel, ctx.
            up_x, ctx.up_y, ctx.down_x, ctx.down_y, ctx.pad_x0, ctx.pad_x1,
            ctx.pad_y0, ctx.pad_y1)
        gradgrad_out = gradgrad_out.view(ctx.in_size[0], ctx.in_size[1],
            ctx.out_size[0], ctx.out_size[1])
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None, None, None, None, None, None


class UpFirDn2d(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, kernel, up, down, pad):
        up_x, up_y = up
        down_x, down_y = down
        pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1 = pad
        kernel_h, kernel_w = kernel.shape
        _batch, channel, in_h, in_w = input.shape
        ctx.in_size = input.shape
        input = input.reshape(-1, in_h, in_w, 1)
        ctx.save_for_backward(kernel, torch.flip(kernel, [0, 1]))
        out_h = (in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1 - kernel_h) // down_y + 1
        out_w = (in_w * up_x + pad_x0 + pad_x1 - kernel_w) // down_x + 1
        ctx.out_size = out_h, out_w
        ctx.up = up_x, up_y
        ctx.down = down_x, down_y
        ctx.pad = pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1
        g_pad_x0 = kernel_w - pad_x0 - 1
        g_pad_y0 = kernel_h - pad_y0 - 1
        g_pad_x1 = in_w * up_x - out_w * down_x + pad_x0 - up_x + 1
        g_pad_y1 = in_h * up_y - out_h * down_y + pad_y0 - up_y + 1
        ctx.g_pad = g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1
        out = upfirdn2d_ext.upfirdn2d(input, kernel, up_x, up_y, down_x,
            down_y, pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1)
        out = out.view(-1, channel, out_h, out_w)
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        kernel, grad_kernel = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input = UpFirDn2dBackward.apply(grad_output, kernel,
            grad_kernel, ctx.up, ctx.down, ctx.pad, ctx.g_pad, ctx.in_size,
            ctx.out_size)
        return grad_input, None, None, None, None

Therefore, to do this with the suffix array, we can construct a virtual array where each element is the suffix starting at position i, and perform a binary search for Y.

When performing a binary search, say we want to find the first index in the suffix array where the suffix starts with Y.

The functions needed are:

Function compare( a, m) : compare Y with s[a:a+m].

To find the lower and upper bounds of the range where Y occurs as a prefix of the suffixes (in the first m characters).

The lower bound: the smallest index such that the suffix starts at SA[index], so that the first m chars is >= Y.

Wait, perhaps to find the lower bound, we can set left=0, right = len(SA). 

Wait, implementing lower and upper bounds would be required.

Alternatively, the code could look like:

def count_occurrences(s, SA, Y, m):

    n = len(s)

    left = 0

    right = n

    # Find the first pos where SA[i]'s suffix has the first m chars =Y.

    low = 0

    high = n - 1

    first = -1

    while low <= high:

        mid = (low + high) //2

        pos = SA[mid]

        if s[pos:pos+m] == Y and pos +m <=n:

            high = mid -1

            first = mid

        else:

            if s[pos:pos+m] < Y:

                low = mid +1

            else: 

                high = mid-1

    Similarly, find the upper bound.

Wait this would involve comparing substrings each time. Each comparison of a substring Y vs s['st..'] would take O(m) time. 

Since m can be up to 1e6, and for each query this is O(m log N), this would be O(m * 20) time for each query. 

Suppose for example, the sum of all m across queries is 1e6, then the total operation count would be 1e6 * 20 * log (1e6) ≈ 1e7 * (20 * 20?), which is under a billion operations. Hmm, that might be a bit tight, but maybe manageable.

Alternatively, instead of doing the substring comparisons, perhaps we can precompute a prefix hash array for s allowing for O(1) comparisons.

Suppose that for s, we have a prefix array which can hash the substring s[pos ... pos + L - 1] in O(1) time for any pos and L. 

So for comparing a substring of length m, starting at pos with Y's hash, we can compute that hash quickly. 

Let me explain this way:

Precompute for s:

- prefix_hash1 array where each position holds the rolling hash for the substring up to that point.

- And precompute power arrays for the base mod.

Then, the hash of Y is precomputed once, and the hash of s's substring starting at position i, of length m is computed in O(1) time. Comparing two strings' equality can be approximated by comparing their hash values. (Assuming minimal collision chance.)

This would reduce the binary search to O(log N) time per query (after precomputing the hashes.)

Thus steps would be:

Precompute the rolling hash array for s.

For a query Y (precomputed), hash is H:

- find the lower and upper bounds in the suffix array's positions where the hash of the substring equals H.

class UpFirDnSmooth(nn.Module):
    """Upsample, FIR filter, and downsample (smooth version).

    Args:
        resample_kernel (list[int]): A list indicating the 1D resample kernel
            magnitude.
        upsample_factor (int): Upsampling scale factor. Default: 1.
        downsample_factor (int): Downsampling scale factor. Default: 1.
        kernel_size (int): Kernel size: Deafult: 1.
    """

    def __init__(self, resample_kernel, upsample_factor=1,
        downsample_factor=1, kernel_size=1):
        super(UpFirDnSmooth, self).__init__()
        self.upsample_factor = upsample_factor
        self.downsample_factor = downsample_factor
        self.kernel = make_resample_kernel(resample_kernel)
        if upsample_factor > 1:
            self.kernel = self.kernel * upsample_factor ** 2
        if upsample_factor > 1:
            pad = self.kernel.shape[0] - upsample_factor - (kernel_size - 1)
            self.pad = (pad + 1) // 2 + upsample_factor - 1, pad // 2 + 1
        elif downsample_factor > 1:
            pad = self.kernel.shape[0] - downsample_factor + (kernel_size - 1)
            self.pad = (pad + 1) // 2, pad // 2
        else:
            raise NotImplementedError

    def forward(self, x):
        out = upfirdn2d(x, self.kernel.type_as(x), up=1, down=1, pad=self.pad)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}(upsample_factor={self.upsample_factor}, downsample_factor={self.downsample_factor})'
            )


class FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, out, negative_slope, scale):
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        empty = grad_output.new_empty(0)
        grad_input = fused_act_ext.fused_bias_act(grad_output, empty, out, 
            3, 1, negative_slope, scale)
        dim = [0]
        if grad_input.ndim > 2:
            dim += list(range(2, grad_input.ndim))
        grad_bias = grad_input.sum(dim).detach()
        return grad_input, grad_bias

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input, gradgrad_bias):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_out = fused_act_ext.fused_bias_act(gradgrad_input,
            gradgrad_bias, out, 3, 1, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None


class FusedLeakyReLUFunction(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, bias, negative_slope, scale):
        empty = input.new_empty(0)
        out = fused_act_ext.fused_bias_act(input, bias, empty, 3, 0,
            negative_slope, scale)
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input, grad_bias = FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward.apply(
            grad_output, out, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return grad_input, grad_bias, None, None


class EqualLinear(nn.Module):
    """Equalized Linear as StyleGAN2.

    Args:
        in_channels (int): Size of each sample.
        out_channels (int): Size of each output sample.
        bias (bool): If set to ``False``, the layer will not learn an additive
            bias. Default: ``True``.
        bias_init_val (float): Bias initialized value. Default: 0.
        lr_mul (float): Learning rate multiplier. Default: 1.
        activation (None | str): The activation after ``linear`` operation.
            Supported: 'fused_lrelu', None. Default: None.
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, bias=True, bias_init_val=
        0, lr_mul=1, activation=None):
        super(EqualLinear, self).__init__()
        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.out_channels = out_channels
        self.lr_mul = lr_mul
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation not in ['fused_lrelu', None]:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Wrong activation value in EqualLinear: {activation}Supported ones are: ['fused_lrelu', None]."
                )
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(in_channels) * lr_mul
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(out_channels, in_channels).
            div_(lr_mul))
        if bias:
            self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(out_channels).fill_(
                bias_init_val))
        else:
            self.register_parameter('bias', None)

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.bias is None:
            bias = None
        else:
            bias = self.bias * self.lr_mul
        if self.activation == 'fused_lrelu':
            out = F.linear(x, self.weight * self.scale)
            out = fused_leaky_relu(out, bias)
        else:
            out = F.linear(x, self.weight * self.scale, bias=bias)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}(in_channels={self.in_channels}, out_channels={self.out_channels}, bias={self.bias is not None})'
            )


class ModulatedConv2d(nn.Module):
    """Modulated Conv2d used in StyleGAN2.

    There is no bias in ModulatedConv2d.

    Args:
        in_channels (int): Channel number of the input.
        out_channels (int): Channel number of the output.
        kernel_size (int): Size of the convolving kernel.
        num_style_feat (int): Channel number of style features.
        demodulate (bool): Whether to demodulate in the conv layer.
            Default: True.
        sample_mode (str | None): Indicating 'upsample', 'downsample' or None.
            Default: None.
        resample_kernel (list[int]): A list indicating the 1D resample kernel
            magnitude. Default: (1, 3, 3, 1).
        eps (float): A value added to the denominator for numerical stability.
            Default: 1e-8.
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size,
        num_style_feat, demodulate=True, sample_mode=None, resample_kernel=
        (1, 3, 3, 1), eps=1e-08):
        super(ModulatedConv2d, self).__init__()
        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.out_channels = out_channels
        self.kernel_size = kernel_size
        self.demodulate = demodulate
        self.sample_mode = sample_mode
        self.eps = eps
        if self.sample_mode == 'upsample':
            self.smooth = UpFirDnSmooth(resample_kernel, upsample_factor=2,
                downsample_factor=1, kernel_size=kernel_size)
        elif self.sample_mode == 'downsample':
            self.smooth = UpFirDnSmooth(resample_kernel, upsample_factor=1,
                downsample_factor=2, kernel_size=kernel_size)
        elif self.sample_mode is None:
            pass
        else:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Wrong sample mode {self.sample_mode}, supported ones are ['upsample', 'downsample', None]."
                )
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(in_channels * kernel_size ** 2)
        self.modulation = EqualLinear(num_style_feat, in_channels, bias=
            True, bias_init_val=1, lr_mul=1, activation=None)
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(1, out_channels, in_channels,
            kernel_size, kernel_size))
        self.padding = kernel_size // 2

    def forward(self, x, style):
        """Forward function.

        Args:
            x (Tensor): Tensor with shape (b, c, h, w).
            style (Tensor): Tensor with shape (b, num_style_feat).

        Returns:
            Tensor: Modulated tensor after convolution.
        """
        b, c, h, w<#sep#> and e_i such that d_i + e_{i-1} = d_i', with e_i being digits (0 ≤ e_i ≤ m-1). So for each digit d_i', starting from i=1, we can choose e_{i-1} such that 0 ≤ e_{i-1} ≤ m-1, and then d_i = d_i' - e_{i-1}. However, d_i must also be between 0 and m-1. Therefore, for each i ≥1, given d_i', the value of e_{i-1} can be chosen such that 0 ≤ d_i' - e_{i-1} ≤ m-1. Therefore, e_{i-1} can range from max(0, d_i' - (m-1)) to min(m-1, d_i').

d_i = d_i' - e_{i-1}

0 ≤ d_i' - e_{i-1} ≤ m-1

Rearranged:

d_i' - (m -1) ≤ e_{i-1} ≤ d_i'

But e_{i-1} must also be between 0 and m -1. Therefore, combining these:

max(0, d_i' - (m -1)) ≤ e_{i-1} ≤ min(m -1, d_i')

So the number of possible choices for e_{i-1} is min(m -1, d_i') - max(0, d_i' - (m -1)) + 1.

Case 2: d_i' ≥ m.

Number of e_{i-1}: 2 +1=3. e_{i-1}=0,1,2. Then d_i=2 -0=2, 2-1=1, 2-2=0. All valid.

Yes, this makes sense. Therefore, in general, for each digit d_i' in the base m expansion of n (i ≥1), the number of possible choices for e_{i-1} is d_i' +1. 

Now, moving to the higher coefficients. After solving for e_{i-1}, we can then compute d_i = d_i' - e_{i-1}. However, we must also consider the next digit. We have:

For each i from 0 to t:

If i=0: d_0 = d_0'

And e_i can be any value such that 0 ≤ e_i ≤ m -1, but e_i will influence the next digit.
