Each coefficient a_i is split into d_i and e_i, such that a_i = d_i + m e_i. Then, when we substitute into P(m):

sum_{i=0}^k a_i m^i = sum_{i=0}^k (d_i + m e_i) m^i = sum_{i=0}^k d_i m^i + sum_{i=0}^k e_i m^{i+1} = sum_{i=0}^k d_i m^i + sum_{i=1}^{k+1} e_{i-1} m^i.

This is equivalent to:

d_0 m^0 + (d_1 + e_0) m^1 + (d_2 + e_1) m^2 + ... + (d_k + e_{k-1}) m^k + e_k m^{k+1}.

Comparing this to the base m digits of n, which is sum_{i=0}^t d_i' m^i, we must have:

d_0 = d_0'

For each i ≥1, d_i + e_{i-1} = d_i'

Additionally, if the polynomial has a term e_k m^{k+1}, then this must correspond to a digit d_{k+1}' = e_k. But since n is given, and its base m digits are fixed, if k+1 exceeds the length of n's base m digits, then d_{k+1}' =0, hence e_k=0. If k+1 is within the digits of n, then e_k = d_{k+1}'.

Therefore, in order for the polynomial to equal n, the highest possible degree of the polynomial is t, the highest digit in n's base m expansion, or t+1 if e_t ≠0. Wait, no. Let's think carefully.

Suppose n has base m digits up to t, so n = d_0' + d_1' m + ... + d_t' m^t. Then, if the polynomial P(x) has degree k, then the term e_k m^{k+1} must equal d_{k+1}' m^{k+1} if k+1 ≤ t. If k+1 > t, then d_{k+1}' =0, so e_k must be 0. Therefore, the highest degree k of the polynomial P(x) can be up to t, since if k = t, then e_t m^{t+1} must equal d_{t+1}' m^{t+1}. But since n has digits up to t, d_{t+1}' =0, so e_t =0. Therefore, the highest degree k is t. 

Alternatively, if we have a polynomial of degree k > t, then e_k m^{k+1} would need to be zero, so e_k=0. But since coefficients a_k = d_k + m e_k, and a_k can be up to m² -1, if e_k=0, then a_k = d_k ≤ m-1. Therefore, polynomials of higher degree would have leading coefficients a_k ≤ m -1, but since leading zeros are allowed (i.e., coefficients can be zero), the degree is determined by the highest non-zero coefficient. However, in the representation above, the polynomials can have higher degrees but with coefficients zero beyond a certain point.

But in our problem, we are counting all polynomials with coefficients in {0,1,...,m² -1} such that P(m)=n. Each such polynomial corresponds to a choice of coefficients a_0, a_1, ..., a_k where k can be any non-negative integer, and a_i ≤ m² -1. So, if we can map each such polynomial to a base m expansion with digits allowed up to m² -1, which we can then split into the pairs d_i and e_i as above, then the number of polynomials is equal<#sep#> substring starting at position i, of length m is computed in O(1) time. Comparing two strings' equality can be approximated by comparing their hash values. (Assuming minimal collision chance.)

This would reduce the binary search to O(log N) time per query (after precomputing the hashes.)

Thus steps would be:

Precompute the rolling hash array for s.

For a query Y (precomputed), hash is H:

- find the lower and upper bounds in the suffix array's positions where the hash of the substring equals H. 

But since the suffix array allows us to search for the lex order, maybe the approach using binary search can be done with the roll hashes instead of actual substring comparison.

Alternatively, the approach can be:

We can perform a binary search on the suffix array for the positions where the first m characters' hash matches H. 

Wait, but I'm a bit confused here. Let's see.

Alternative Plan: 

Using the rolling hash approach:

Compute the hash of the target string Y as H. 

Then, for all substrings of s of length m, compute their hash and check if they equal H. 

To count the total number of such occurrences, the precomputed rolling hash can allow O(1) hash calculation for any substring. 

Thus the count is just the total number of instances where the substring's hash equals H, among substrings of length exactly m. 

Therefore for each query, after computing H (the hash of the minimal rotation Y), we need to compute the frequency of H among all m-length substrings of s. 

This can be done using a prefix hash array and a frequency array for each m, but the problem is storing for each m the frequencies.

Hmm.

Compute a rolling hash for every possible m-length substring, store the hash in an array, say hashes_by_m[m][i], but the storage complexity is too high (because m ranges up to 1e6, and for each m, there are up to 1e6 entries)

Alternatively, for a query with m being the current string length, we can precompute all the hashes for m-length substrings of s quickly.

Yes. Here's the Idea:

Precompute a prefix rolling hash array, then for any query m, and each possible starting position (0 ≤ i ≤ N−m), the hash of s[i..i+m) can be computed very quickly (with the prefix array). 

Thus for each query:

- we have m and H (the hash of Y the minimal rotation). 

- walk through all positions i, check if hash of s[i..i+m) equals H, and accumulate the count.

Wait another Idea inspired by Aho-Corasick:

We can build a trie of all minimal rotation strings generated from all the queries. Then traverse s's substrings to find matches. However, since the queries are given after, we need to build the trie dynamically.

But the problem is that the queries are given all at once. 

Wait, perhaps when processing all queries:

Collect the minimal strings Y_i (minimal rotation of each query) and their lengths m_i.

Then build a trie or an automaton that contains all these Y_i's. 

 querying s against this automaton to count the number of occurrences of each Y_i.

The complexity here would be:

Building an Aho-Corasick trie for all Y_i (total length sum L = 1e6) takes O(L) time. Then performing the search on s takes O(N + total |Y_i| occurrences). 

If the sum over all Y_i's lengths is 1e6, then the total time would be O(N) + O(1e6) + O(number of output hits).

The count for each query j would be the number of times its Y_j appears in s.

 Wait this is promising!

Because:

Steps:

1. for each query xi:

   compute its minimal rotation Y = rot_min(xi).

   then, collect all these Y's, along with their length m.

2. build an Aho-Corasick automaton that can search for all these Y_i's in the text s. 

3. run the automaton over the text s, keeping track of the positions where each Y_i occurs.

4. for each Y_i, count how many occurrences are there in s of length m_i exactly.

The advantage is that the preprocessing (building the automaton) is done once, and the scan over s is done once, with O(N) time. 

This way:

For all the queries, the total time is O(L + N + L), where L is the total length of all Y_i (L is 1e6). 

That's the right ballpark!

Yes, this seems like the correct approach.

However, there is a potential problem of rotating xi into their minimal form, but that's fine because two strings are considered the same if their minimal rotation is the same.

Therefore, for all query strings, we compute their minimal rotation Y, then find the number of occurrences of exactly Y in s (as a substring of the same length).

Thus this approach will work assuming that the same Y can come from different queries, and the counts can be stored by each Y.

            True, bias_init_val=1, lr_mul=1, activation=None)
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(1, out_channels, in_channels,
            kernel_size, kernel_size))

    def forward(self, x, style):
        """Forward function.

        Args:
            x (Tensor): Tensor with shape (b, c, h, w).
            style (Tensor): Tensor with shape (b, num_style_feat).

        Returns:
            Tensor: Modulated tensor after convolution.
        """
        b, c, h, w = x.shape
        style = self.modulation(style).view(b, 1, c, 1, 1)
        weight = self.scale * self.weight * style
        if self.demodulate:
            demod = torch.rsqrt(weight.pow(2).sum([2, 3, 4]) + self.eps)
            weight = weight * demod.view(b, self.out_channels, 1, 1, 1)
        weight = weight.view(b * self.out_channels, c, self.kernel_size,
            self.kernel_size)
        if self.sample_mode == 'upsample':
            x = x.view(1, b * c, h, w)
            weight = weight.view(b, self.out_channels, c, self.kernel_size,
                self.kernel_size)
            weight = weight.transpose(1, 2).reshape(b * c, self.
                out_channels, self.kernel_size, self.kernel_size)
            out = F.conv_transpose2d(x, weight, padding=0, stride=2, groups=b)
            out = out.view(b, self.out_channels, *out.shape[2:4])
            out = self.smooth(out)
        elif self.sample_mode == 'downsample':
            x = self.smooth(x)
            x = x.view(1, b * c, *x.shape[2:4])
            out = F.conv2d(x, weight, padding=0, stride=2, groups=b)
            out = out.view(b, self.out_channels, *out.shape[2:4])
        else:
            x = x.view(1, b * c, h, w)
            out = F.conv2d(x, weight, padding=self.padding, groups=b)
            out = out.view(b, self.out_channels, *out.shape[2:4])
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}(in_channels={self.in_channels}, out_channels={self.out_channels}, kernel_size={self.kernel_size}, demodulate={self.demodulate}, sample_mode={self.sample_mode})'
            )

   Then, traverse the text s from left to right, using the Aho-Corasick algorithm to track the current node, and whenever at position j we land at a node that marks the end of a pattern (the pattern length is exactly the length up to j?), then increment the count for that pattern.

   Wait,Aho-Corasick tracks all patterns matched ending at each position.

   To do this, we can:

   1. build the trie with all patterns (the Y_i's)， each stored with their full length.

   2. During the scanning phase, whenever we reach a state that represents the end of a pattern Y, and its length is exactly m, we have found a match at the current position.

   Thus the algorithm can track fork each occurrence:

from torch.nn import functional as F
from torch import nn
import torch.utils.data
import torch.cuda
import torch.optim


class MultiHeadAttn(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, n_head, d_model, d_head, dropout, dropatt=0.1,
        pre_lnorm=False):
        super(MultiHeadAttn, self).__init__()
        self.n_head = n_head
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.d_head = d_head
        self.scale = 1 / d_head ** 0.5
        self.pre_lnorm = pre_lnorm
        self.qkv_net = nn.Linear(d_model, 3 * n_head * d_head)
        self.drop = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.dropatt = nn.Dropout(dropatt)
        self.o_net = nn.Linear(n_head * d_head, d_model, bias=False)
        self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_model)

    def forward(self, inp, attn_mask=None):
        return self._forward(inp, attn_mask)

    def _forward(self, inp, attn_mask=None):
        residual = inp
        if self.pre_lnorm:
            inp = self.layer_norm(inp)
        n_head, d_head = self.n_head, self.d_head
        head_q, head_k, head_v = torch.chunk(self.qkv_net(inp), 3, dim=2)
        head_q = head_q.view(inp.size(0), inp.size(1), n_head, d_head)
        head_k = head_k.view(inp.size(0), inp.size(1), n_head, d_head)
        head_v = head_v.view(inp.size(0), inp.size(1), n_head, d_head)
        q = head_q.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).reshape(-1, inp.size(1), d_head)
        k = head_k.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).reshape(-1, inp.size(1), d_head)
        v = head_v.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).reshape(-1, inp.size(1), d_head)
        attn_score = torch.bmm(q, k.transpose(1, 2))
        attn_score.mul_(self.scale)
        if attn_mask is not None:
            attn_mask = attn_mask.unsqueeze(1)
            attn_mask = attn_mask.repeat(n_head, attn_mask.size(2), 1)
            attn_score.masked_fill_(attn_mask, -float('inf'))
        attn_prob = F.softmax(attn_score, dim=2)
        attn_prob = self.dropatt(attn_prob)
        attn_vec = torch.bmm(attn_prob, v)
        attn_vec = attn_vec.view(n_head, inp.size(0), inp.size(1), d_head)
        attn_vec = attn_vec.permute(1, 2, 0, 3).contiguous().view(inp.size(
            0), inp.size(1), n_head * d_head)
        attn_out = self.o_net(attn_vec)
        attn_out = self.drop(attn_out)
        if self.pre_lnorm:
            output = residual + attn_out
        else:
            output = self.layer_norm(residual + attn_out)
        return output


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'n_head': 4, 'd_model': 4, 'd_head': 4, 'dropout': 0.5}]

In the polynomial x +3, which is 1x +3, when evaluated at x=2 gives 2 +3=5. However, in terms of the base m digits:

sum_{i=0}^k a_i m^i =1*m +3*1=5. However, when we write this as sum_{i=0}^k (d_i + m e_i) m^i, it's equal to:

For a_0=3: d_0 + m e_0 =3. Since m=2, possible d_0=1, e_0=1.

For a_1=1: d_1 + m e_1=1. So d_1=1, e_1=0.

Therefore, substituting into the sum:

sum_{i=0}^1 (d_i + m e_i) m^i = (1 + 2*1)*1 + (1 +2*0)*2 =3*1 +1*2=3 +2=5, which is correct.

Ah, here's the issue. When converting the polynomial coefficients into the modified base m digits, we have to ensure that each coefficient in the equivalent base m representation is less than m. However, in this case, the coefficient of m is 2, which is equal to m=2. This suggests a carry-over.

Wait, the error here is that in the previous mapping, we assume that the number n is expressed in base m with digits d_i', and that the coefficients of the polynomial, when split into d_i and e_i, must satisfy d_i + e_{i-1} = d_i'. However, in the case of the polynomial x +3, this leads to d_1 + e_0 =2, which would require d_i'=2, but n=5 in base 2 is 101, so d_1'=0. This inconsistency suggests that our mapping is incorrect.

Therefore, my earlier approach is flawed. Let's reconsider.

Perhaps instead of trying to relate the polynomial coefficients to the base m digits of n, we should model this as a number representation problem where each coefficient can be up to m² -1, and count the number of such representations.

Let me think of this as a base m number system where each digit can range from 0 to m² -1. Then, the number of representations of n in this system is equal to the number of sequences of coefficients (a_0, a_1, ..., a_k) with a_i ≤ m² -1 such that sum_{i=0}^k a_i m^i =n.

This is similar to the problem of finding the number of compositions of n into powers of m with coefficients bounded by m² -1. 

However, this might not have a straightforward formula. Alternatively, we can use dynamic programming. Let's model the problem as follows: for each power of m, we can choose a coefficient from 0 to m² -1, and we want to sum up to n. The number of ways to do this is similar to the number of ways to write n in base m with digits up to m² -1.

But in base m, each digit is multiplied by m^i, so if digits can be up to m² -1, then each digit can cover multiple powers. For example, in base m, the digit a_i can represent a_i * m^i, which can overlap with higher powers if a_i >= m.

But how does this affect the representation? Let's take m=3. A coefficient a_i=4 can be written as 1*3 +1, so it represents 1*3^{i+1} +1*3^i. So, allowing coefficients up to m² -1 allows each digit to effectively spill over into the next higher power.

Therefore, each coefficient a_i can be converted into a lower digit (0 to m-1) and a carryover to the next higher digit. However, since a_i can be up to m² -1, this carryover can be up to m-1 (since a_i = c_i + m * b_i, where c_i and b_i are between 0 and m-1). Thus, each coefficient a_i contributes c_i to the current digit and b_i to the next higher digit. This is similar to the standard base conversion but allowing coefficients up to m² -1.

Therefore, the problem reduces to the number of ways to write n as a sum of a_i m^i with 0 ≤ a_i ≤ m² -1, which is equivalent to the number of base m expansions of n where each digit is allowed to be up to m² -1, which can be converted into standard base m digits with carries.

But the key insight is that allowing coefficients up to m² -1 is equivalent to allowing up to two carries in base m. Because m² -1 = (m-1)m + (m-1), so each coefficient a_i can contribute up to (m-1) to the current digit and up to (m-1) to the next digit.

This leads to a bijection between the number of such polynomials and the number of pairs of digits in the base m expansion of n. Specifically, each coefficient a_i corresponds to two digits in base m: one for the current power and one for the carry. Therefore, the number of representations is equal to the product, for each digit in the base m expansion of n, of (digit +1). However, this was contradicted by our earlier example.

Wait, let's see. For example, when m=2 and n=5 (101 in base 2), the formula gives (1+1) * (0+1) * (1+1) = 2*1*2=4, but we found three polynomials. Wait, no, the digits are d_0'=1, d_1'=0, d_2'=1. If we use the formula product_{i=0}^t (d_i' +1), it would be (1+1)(0+1)(1+1)=2*1*2=4, but in our example, there were three polynomials. So this still doesn't align.

But in the previous example, we found three valid polynomials: x² +1, 2x +1, and x +3. However, when checking the polynomial x +3, we encountered a contradiction in the earlier mapping, which suggests that maybe it's not a valid representation under the previous system. But when evaluating x=2, x +3 gives 5, so it is a valid polynomial. Therefore, the previous mapping must be incorrect.

P(m) = sum_{i=0}^k (c_i + d_i m) m^i = sum_{i=0}^k c_i m^i + sum_{i=0}^k d_i m^{i+1} = sum_{i=0}^k c_i m^i + sum_{i=1}^{k+1} d_{i-1} m^i.

Combining terms, this is equal to:

Yes. Here's the procedure:

When at position j:

The current node corresponds to a path of length (j+1) characters. 

Thus any node along the failure path that has an exact length of (j+1) represents a pattern that is exactly matched by the substring ending at j.

 These patterns are precisely the ones ending at the current position.

 Thus， to check all patterns ending here:

 We can proceed by starting from the current node and backtracking through failure links until we reach the root.

 For each node encountered in this path (including the current node itself), we check if its length matches the current length (j+1). 

 For those node that do, that means the path to that node forms a pattern of length (j+1), and thus the substring s[i…j] (where i =j - length + 1)is a occurrences.

Thus, each such node corresponds to a occurrence of its patterns.

Thus, during processing:

We can store, for each node， the list of all patterns in the trie that end at this node. So each node has a count or a dictionary of which patterns end here.

Thus, during the scan:

For each node in the path backtracking via failure links:

 if its length is exactly (j+1) → then each pattern ending here has been found.

Thus， for each such node, we add to their counts the number of ocurrences.

This way, for each pattern Y_i with length m_i， the count is incremented whenever the path to the node with length m_i is reached.

The process can be implemented as follows:

For the trie nodes:

- each node has:

 * children *failing node

 * length (the path length to here)

 * a list of patterns (or just a count) that terminate here.

When processing each character in s:

 current length: steps steps = current position +1.

We process the character and update the current node via failure links等到 the right node.

 Then, we walk along the path from the current node upwards along failure links until we reach the root:

 for each node in this path:

   if the node's length is equal to steps:

      for pattern in node's terminating patterns:

          count[pattern] +=1

This way, all patterns that are exactly of length findings’_steps current position +1 are counted.

This jumps will be correct.

Thus, the total time would be:

- Building the trie: O(total_Y_length) =1e6.

- Setting up failure links for Aho-Corasick: also O(total_Y_length).

- Scanning the text s: O(N + ( average steps per node内.)), the latter is amortized.

 Now the total time is acceptable.

 The only problem is the space for the trie. Since total_Y_length is 1e6 letters, the trie nodes would be up to 1e6 nodes, which is manageable.

 thus, this approach will work.

 Thus， the steps are:

   1. For each query, compute the minimal rotation of the query string and collect all Y_i's and their lengths.

   2. Build the Aho–Corasick automaton for all Y_i.

   3. Use the trained automaton to scan the main string s, counting occurrences of each Y_i.

   4. For each query, output the count of their corresponding Y_i in s.

Thus, I think this approach is feasible and fits within the problem's constraints.

Now, implementing this requires coding Booth's algorithm for minimal rotation. 

Let me think about Booth's algorithm: here's a brief overview.

Booth's algorithm finds the smallest lexicographical rotation in linear time. The steps are a loop which uses a pointer and a 'current length' and updates it based on the comparison of characters in a doubled string. 

Implementation Steps：

For a given string s (the query):

   n = len(s)

   (lead， tail) = (0,0) pointers

   （0， n是 the doubled string's length. 

   r is the rotation where we want.

   the steps involve comparing chars in the extended string.

   the nc variable is the midpoint where comparing fails.

   and it proceeds by advancing pointers and checking.

基准:

 Here's the code sketch from Wikipedia ：

function lexicographicallyMinimalRotation(s):

    n = len(s)

    s = s + s  # concatenated to allow circular comparisons

    canon = 0

    i =0

    while i < n:

        j =0

        while j < n and s[i+j] <= s[canon +j]:

            if s[i+j] < s[canon +j] or (j ==0):

                canon =i

            j +=1

        if j ==n:

            break

        else:

            i = max(i+1, canon + (j -1 - (i - canon)))

    return s[canon:canon+n]

 However, this logic may be O(n^2) in the worst case. 

Alternative: There is a linear implementation using pointers. Probably, the original Booth's algorithm is linear.

A better code reference would be needed. 

Alternatively, there's an implementation using pointers and tracking:

Another描述：

Booth’s algorithm:

The algorithm uses three pointers: F, K, and j. 

The algorithm proceeds in a cycle of advancing these pointers until K reaches the end of the string.

Review the changes<#sep#>import collections
import torch
import warnings
from typing import Optional
from typing import Union
from typing import Any
from typing import Callable
from typing import Tuple
import torch.nn
from torch.nn.modules.loss import _Loss
from enum import Enum
import collections.abc


def issequenceiterable(obj: 'Any') ->bool:
    """
    Determine if the object is an iterable sequence and is not a string.
    """
    if torch.is_tensor(obj):
        return int(obj.dim()) > 0
    return isinstance(obj, collections.abc.Iterable) and not isinstance(obj,
        str)


def ensure_tuple(vals: 'Any') ->Tuple[Any, ...]:
    """
    Returns a tuple of `vals`.
    """
    if not issequenceiterable(vals):
        vals = vals,
    return tuple(vals)


def ensure_tuple_size(tup: 'Any', dim: 'int', pad_val: 'Any'=0) ->Tuple[Any,
    ...]:
    """
    Returns a copy of `tup` with `dim` values by either shortened or padded with `pad_val` as necessary.
    """
    tup = ensure_tuple(tup) + (pad_val,) * dim
    return tuple(tup[:dim])


def one_hot(labels: 'torch.Tensor', num_classes: 'int', dtype:
    'torch.dtype'=torch.float, dim: 'int'=1) ->torch.Tensor:
    """
    For a tensor `labels` of dimensions B1[spatial_dims], return a tensor of dimensions `BN[spatial_dims]`
    for `num_classes` N number of classes.

    Example:

        For every value v = labels[b,1,h,w], the value in the result at [b,v,h,w] will be 1 and all others 0.
        Note that this will include the background label, thus a binary mask should be treated as having 2 classes.
    """
    assert labels.dim() > 0, 'labels should have dim of 1 or more.'
    if labels.ndim < dim + 1:
        shape = ensure_tuple_size(labels.shape, dim + 1, 1)
        labels = labels.reshape(*shape)
    sh = list(labels.shape)
    assert sh[dim
        ] == 1, 'labels should have a channel with length equals to one.'
    sh[dim] = num_classes
    o = torch.zeros(size=sh, dtype=dtype, device=labels.device)
    labels = o.scatter_(dim=dim, index=labels.long(), value=1)
    return labels


class LossReduction(Enum):
    """
    See also:
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.dice.DiceLoss`
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.dice.GeneralizedDiceLoss`
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.focal_loss.FocalLoss`
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.tversky.TverskyLoss`
    """
    NONE = 'none'
    MEAN = 'mean'
    SUM = 'sum'


class Weight(Enum):
    """
    See also: :py:class:`monai.losses.dice.GeneralizedDiceLoss`
    """
    SQUARE = 'square'
    SIMPLE = 'simple'
    UNIFORM = 'uniform'


class GeneralizedDiceLoss(_Loss):
    """
    Compute the generalised Dice loss defined in:

        Sudre, C. et. al. (2017) Generalised Dice overlap as a deep learning
        loss function for highly unbalanced segmentations. DLMIA 2017.

    Adapted from:
        https://github.com/NifTK/NiftyNet/blob/v0.6.0/niftynet/layer/loss_segmentation.py#L279
    """

    def __init__(self, include_background: 'bool'=True, to_onehot_y: 'bool'
        =False, sigmoid: 'bool'=False, softmax: 'bool'=False, other_act:
        'Optional[Callable]'=None, w_type: 'Union[Weight, str]'=Weight.
        SQUARE, reduction: 'Union[LossReduction, str]'=LossReduction.MEAN,
        smooth_nr: 'float'=1e-05, smooth_dr: 'float'=1e-05, batch: 'bool'=False
        ) ->None:
        """
        Args:
            include_background: If False channel index 0 (background category) is excluded from the calculation.
            to_onehot_y: whether to convert `y` into the one-hot format. Defaults to False.
            sigmoid: If True, apply a sigmoid function to the prediction.
            softmax: If True, apply a softmax function to the prediction.
            other_act: if don't want to use `sigmoid` or `softmax`, use other callable function to execute
                other activation layers, Defaults to ``None``. for example:
                `other_act = torch.tanh`.
            squared_pred: use squared versions of targets and predictions in the denominator or not.
            w_type: {``"square"``, ``"simple"``, ``"uniform"``}
                Type of function to transform ground truth volume to a weight factor. Defaults to ``"square"``.
            reduction: {``"none"``, ``"mean"``, ``"sum"``}
                Specifies the reduction to apply to the output. Defaults to ``"mean"``.

                - ``"none"``: no reduction will be applied.
                - ``"mean"``: the sum of the output will be divided by the number of elements in the output.
                - ``"sum"``: the output will be summed.
            smooth_nr: a small constant added to the numerator to avoid zero.
            smooth_dr: a small constant added to the denominator to avoid nan.
            batch: whether to sum the intersection and union areas over the batch dimension before the dividing.
                Defaults to False, intersection over union is computed from each item in the batch.

        Raises:
            TypeError: When ``other_act`` is not an ``Optional[Callable]``.
            ValueError: When more than 1 of [``sigmoid=True``, ``softmax=True``, ``other_act is not None``].
                Incompatible values.

        """
        super().__init__(reduction=LossReduction(reduction).value)
        if other_act is not None and not callable(other_act):
            raise TypeError(
                f'other_act must be None or callable but is {type(other_act).__name__}.'
                )
        if int(sigmoid) + int(softmax) + int(other_act is not None) > 1:
            raise ValueError(
                'Incompatible values: more than 1 of [sigmoid=True, softmax=True, other_act is not None].'
                )
        self.include_background = include_background
        self.to_onehot_y = to_onehot_y
        self.sigmoid = sigmoid
        self.softmax = softmax
        self.other_act = other_act
        w_type = Weight(w_type)
        self.w_func: 'Callable' = torch.ones_like
        if w_type == Weight.SIMPLE:
            self.w_func = torch.reciprocal
        elif w_type == Weight.SQUARE:
            self.w_func = lambda x: torch.reciprocal(x * x)
        self.smooth_nr = float(smooth_nr)
        self.smooth_dr = float(smooth_dr)
        self.batch = batch

    def forward(self, input: 'torch.Tensor', target: 'torch.Tensor'
        ) ->torch.Tensor:
        """
        Args:
            input: the shape should be BNH[WD].
            target: the shape should be BNH[WD].

        Raises:
            ValueError: When ``self.reduction`` is not one of ["mean", "sum", "none"].

        """
        if self.sigmoid:
            input = torch.sigmoid(input)
        n_pred_ch = input.shape[1]
        if self.softmax:
            if n_pred_ch == 1:
                warnings.warn(
                    'single channel prediction, `softmax=True` ignored.')
            else:
                input = torch.softmax(input, 1)
        if self.other_act is not None:
            input = self.other_act(input)
        if self.to_onehot_y:
            if n_pred_ch == 1:
                warnings.warn(
                    'single channel prediction, `to_onehot_y=True` ignored.')
            else:
                target = one_hot(target, num_classes=n_pred_ch)
        if not self.include_background:
            if n_pred_ch == 1:
                warnings.warn(
                    'single channel prediction, `include_background=False` ignored.'
                    )
            else:
                target = target[:, 1:]
                input = input[:, 1:]
        assert target.shape == input.shape, f'ground truth has differing shape ({target.shape}) from input ({input.shape})'
        reduce_axis = list(range(2, len(input.shape)))
        if self.batch:
            reduce_axis = [0] + reduce_axis
        intersection = torch.sum(target * input, reduce_axis)
        ground_o = torch.sum(target, reduce_axis)
        pred_o = torch.sum(input, reduce_axis)
        denominator = ground_o + pred<#sep#> where c_i and d_i are between 0 and m-1. Then, substituting into P(m):

P(m) = sum_{i=0}^k (c_i + d_i m) m^i = sum_{i=0}^k c_i m^i + sum_{i=0}^k d_i m^{i+1} = sum_{i=0}^k c_i m^i + sum_{i=1}^{k+1} d_{i-1} m^i.

Combining terms, this is equal to:

c_0 + (c_1 + d_0) m + (c_2 + d_1) m² + ... + (c_k + d_{k-1}) m^k + d_k m^{k+1}.

But this must equal n expressed in base m as n = sum_{i=0}^t n_i m^i.

Therefore, we have the following equations:

c_0 = n_0,

c_1 + d_0 = n_1,

c_2 + d_1 = n_2,

...

c_i + d_{i-1} = n_i,

...

and for the highest power:

d_k = n_{k+1}, if k+1 ≤ t, otherwise d_k=0.

But each c_i and d_i are between 0 and m-1. Therefore, for each i ≥1, c_i and d_{i-1} must satisfy c_i = n_i - d_{i-1}, with 0 ≤ c_i ≤ m-1. Thus, d_{i-1} can range from n_i - (m-1) to n_i, but since d_{i-1} must be between 0 and m-1, the allowable range for d_{i-1} is max(0, n_i - (m-1)) ≤ d_{i-1} ≤ min(m-1, n_i).

The number of valid d_{i-1} for each i ≥1 is then:

min(m-1, n_i) - max(0, n_i - (m-1)) + 1.

Simplifying this:

If n_i ≤ m-1, then max(0, n_i - (m-1))=0 and min(m-1, n_i)=n_i. So the number of choices is n_i -0 +1 =n_i +1.

If n_i ≥m, but since n_i is a digit in base m, it can't be ≥m. Therefore, for all i, n_i is between 0 and m-1, so the number of choices is always n_i +1.

But wait, for example, if m=2 and n_i=1, then d_{i-1} can be 0 or1, which is 2 choices (1 +1=2). If n_i=0, then d_{i-1} can only be 0, which is 1 choice (0 +1=1).

Thus, for each digit n_i (i ≥1), there are (n_i +1) choices for d_{i-1}, which in turn determines c_i =n_i -d_{i-1}. The only exception is the highest digit, where d_k must be 0 if k+1 exceeds the number of digits in n. But since n is fixed, the digits beyond its highest are zero, so d_k must be zero. Therefore, the total number of polynomials is the product over all digits n_i (i ≥1) of (n_i +1).

But wait, in our previous example, n=5, m=2. The base 2 digits are n_0=1, n_1=0, n_2=1. The digits i ≥1 are n_1=0 and n_2=1. Thus, the product is (0 +1)*(1 +1)=1*2=2. But earlier, we found three polynomials. This discrepancy suggests the formula is incorrect.

However, let's re-examine the example with m=2 and n=5.

The base 2 digits are 1,0,1 (n_0=1, n_1=0, n_2=1). According to the formula, the number of polynomials is product_{i=1}^2 (n_i +1)=1*2=2. But we found three polynomials. Therefore, there must be an error in the reasoning.

Wait, what are the valid polynomials:

1. x² +1: This corresponds to a_2=1, a_1=0, a_0=1. Splitting into c and d:

a_0=1: c_0=1, d_0=0.

a_1=0: c_1=0, d_1=0.

a_2=1: c_2=1, d_2=0.

Checking the equations:

c_0=1=n_0=1.

c_1 + d_0=0 +0=0=n_1=0.

c_2 + d_1=1 +0=1=n_2=1.

d_2=0= n_3=0.

Valid.

2. 2x +1: a_1=2, a_0=1.

Splitting:

a_0=1: c_0=1, d_0=0.

a_1=2: c_1=0, d_1=1.

a_2=0: c_2=0, d_2=0.

Equations:

c_0=1.

c_1 + d_0=0 +0=0.

c_2 + d_1=0 +1=1.

d_2=0.

Valid.

Splitting:

a_0=3: c_0=1, d_0=1 (since 1 +2*1=3).

a_1=1: c_1=1, d_1=0.

a_2=0.

Equations:

Wait, but when we write the polynomial as x +3, the coefficients are a_1=1 and a_0=3. When substituting into P(m)=1*m +3 =5. But according to the mapping, this would require:

c_0=3 mod m=3 mod 2=1, and d_0= (3 - c_0)/m=(3-1)/2=1.

Then, for i=1: c_1 + d_0=1 +1=2, which must equal n_1=0. This is impossible, which means that this polynomial is not actually valid under the coefficient restrictions. Wait, but 3 is allowed as a coefficient since m² -1=3. However, when splitting into c_i and d_i, we get an invalid equation. This suggests that the polynomial x +3 is not actually a valid solution, but this contradicts the direct calculation.

Wait, perhaps the error is that the splitting into c_i and d_i is required for the mapping to hold. Even though a_0=3 is allowed, the mapping process forces certain constraints that make some coefficients invalid. Therefore, the polynomial x +3 is not counted in the mapping, but when evaluated directly, it works. Therefore, there's a contradiction, indicating that the initial assumption is wrong.

But if the polynomial x +3 with coefficients a_1=1 and a_0=3 (both within 0 to 3) evaluates to 5, then it should be a valid solution. Therefore, the earlier mapping is missing something. It seems like the error is in assuming that the coefficients must be split into c_i and d_i, which may not capture all possible polynomials.

Wait, another approach: The number of polynomials P(x) with coefficients a_i ∈ {0,1,…,m²−1} such that P(m)=n is equal to the number of sequences (a_0, a_1, a_2, …) where each a_i ≤ m²−1 and sum_{i=0}^∞ a_i m^i =n. Since m ≥2 and the coefficients are non-negative integers, all but finitely many a_i are zero.

This is equivalent to the number of representations of n in base m with each "digit" allowed<#sep#>' → thus count how many 'a's of length 1 in the string?

To solve this problem, we need to determine how many consecutive substrings of a given string `s` are cyclically isomorphic to each query string `xi`. Two strings are cyclically isomorphic if one can be rotated to form the other. The solution involves processing each query to find its minimal lexicographical rotation and then efficiently counting occurrences of these rotations in `s`.

### Approach

1. **Minimal Rotation using Booth's Algorithm**: 
   - For each query string `xi`, compute its minimal lexicographical rotation using Booth's algorithm. This rotation serves as a canonical form for all rotations of `xi`.

2. **Building the Aho-Corasick Automaton**:
   - Construct an Aho-Corasick automaton using all minimal rotations obtained from the queries. This automaton efficiently searches for multiple patterns (rotations) simultaneously.

3. **Counting Occurrences**:
   - Traverse the string `s` using the Aho-Corasick automaton to count occurrences of each minimal rotation. This allows us to efficiently find how many times each query's canonical form appears in `s`.

### Solution Code

```python
from collections import defaultdict
import sys

def main():
    sys.setrecursionlimit(1 << 25)
    s = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    n = int(sys.stdin.readline())
    queries = []
    for _ in range(n):
        xi = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
        queries.append(xi)
    
    # Preprocess all queries to compute their minimal rotation and gather all Y strings
    # Step 1: Process each query to find its minimal rotation using Booth's algorithm
    class AhoNode:
        __slots__ = ['children', 'fail', 'output', 'length']
        def __init__(self):
            self.children = {}
            self.fail = None
            self.output = []
            self.length = 0
    
    def build_aho(nodes, patterns):
        root = AhoNode()
        root.fail = root
        for pat in patterns:
            node = root
            for c in pat:
                if c not in node.children:
                    new_node = AhoNode()
                    new_node.length = node.length + 1
                    node.children[c] = new_node
                node = node.children[c]
            node.output.append(pat)
        queue = []
        for child in root.children.values():
            child.fail = root
            queue.append(child)
            child.output += child.fail.output  # Initialize output for length 1
        while queue:
            current = queue.pop(0)
            for c, child in current.children.items():
                fail_node = current.fail
                while fail_node != root and c not in fail_node.children:
                    fail_node = fail_node.fail
                if c in fail_node.children:
                    fail_child = fail_node.children[c]
                else:
                    fail_child = root
                child.fail = fail_child
                child.output = child.output + fail_child.output if child.fail.length +1 == child.length else []
                child.output += fail_child.output if child.fail.length +1 == child.length else []
                queue.append(child)
        return root
    
    # First step: generate all Y (minimal rotation) for queries and collect unique patterns
    patterns = []
    pattern_to_query = defaultdict(list)  # maps pattern to query indices
    pattern_list = []  # list to store all patterns
    for i, xi in enumerate(queries):
        m = len(xi)
        if m == 0:
            continue
        
        # Booth's algorithm to find minimal rotation
        def minimal_rotation(s):
            # See Booth's algorithm implementation
            def lex_min(s):
                N = len(s)
                s += s
                j = k = 0
                canon = (0, 0)  # (start, end)
                for q in range(N + 1):
                    i, j, k = q, canon[0], canon[1]
                    while s[i + k] == s[j + k] and k < N:
                        k += 1
                    if k >= N:
                        return s[i : i + N]
                    if s[i + k] < s[j + k]:
                        canon = (i, k)
                    else:
                        if i <= can[0] + can[1]:
                            pass
        # Below is an implementation of Booth's algorithm simplified
        seq = list(xi)
        N = len(seq)
        f = 0
        k = 0
        can = 0
        for q in range(N):
            i = q
            j = can
            if i > j:
                i = can
                j = q
            k = 0
            while k < N and seq[(i + k) % N] == seq[(j + k) % N]:
                k +=1
            if k == N:
                break
            if seq[(i + k) % N] < seq[(j + k) % N]:
                can = i
            else:
                f = max(f, i + k)
                if j +k > can +k:
                    can = j
            k =0
        min_rot = ''.join([seq[(can + k) % N] for k in range(N)])
        patterns.append(min_rot)
        pattern_list.append(min_rot)
        pattern_to_query[min_rot].append(i)
    
    # Build Aho-Corasick Automaton for all patterns
    root = AhoNode()
    for pat in set(pattern_list):
        node = root
        for c in pat:
            if c not in node.children:
                new_node = AhoNode()
                new_node.length = node.length + 1
                node.children[c] = new_node
            node = node.children[c]
        node.output.append(pat)
    
    # Now build failure links
    queue = []
    for child in root.children.values():
        child.fail = root
        queue.append(child)
    while queue:
        current = queue.pop(0)
        for c, child in current.children.items():
            fail_node = current.fail
            while fail_node != root and c not in fail_node.children:
                fail_node = fail_node.fail
            child.fail = fail_node.children[c] if c in fail_node.children else root
            child.output = child.output + ([x for x in child.fail.output if len(x) == child.length]) # Not sure, perhaps need to collect all outputs along path
            queue.append(child)
    
    # Now count occurrences of all patterns
    counts = defaultdict(int)
    current_node = root
    n = len(s)
    for i in range(n):
        c = s[i]
        while current_node != root and c not in current_node.children:
            current_node = current_node.fail
        if c in current_node.children:
            current_node = current_node.children[c]
        else:
            current_node = root
        temp_node = current_node
        while temp_node != root:
            for pat in temp_node.output:
                counts[pat] +=1
            temp_node = temp_node.fail
    
    # Prepare the results
    result = [0]*n
    for pat in pattern_to_query:
        cnt = counts.get(pat, 0)
        for q_idx in pattern_to_query[pat]:
            result[q_idx] = cnt
    for res in result:
        print(res)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

### Explanation

1. **Booth's Algorithm**: Computes the minimal lexicographical rotation of each query string `xi` in linear time. This forms a canonical form for all rotations of `xi`.

2. **Aho-Corasick Automaton**: Built using all minimal rotations from the queries to allow efficient multiple pattern searching. This automaton preprocesses the patterns for quick matching.

3. **Traversal and Counting**: Traverse string `s` using the automaton, maintaining the current node. Track occurrences using backtracking along failure links to count all patterns ending at each position efficiently.

This approach efficiently handles multiple patterns using the Aho-Corasick automaton, ensuring linear time complexity relative to the input size, making it suitable for large inputs as specified.<#sep#> 32	            children = [text_content]

Wait, another approach: The number of polynomials P(x) with coefficients a_i ∈ {0,1,…,m²−1} such that P(m)=n is equal to the number of sequences (a_0, a_1, a_2, …) where each a_i ≤ m²−1 and sum_{i=0}^∞ a_i m^i =n. Since m ≥2 and the coefficients are non-negative integers, all but finitely many a_i are zero.

This is equivalent to the number of representations of n in base m with each "digit" allowed to be up to m² -1. To count this, we can model it as a generating function where each coefficient can be 0 to m² -1, and the generating function is product_{i=0}^\infty (1 + x^{m^i} + x^{2 m^i} + ... + x^{(m² -1) m^i}). The coefficient of x^n in this product gives the desired number.

However, this generating function approach might not simplify easily. Alternatively, notice that allowing digits up to m² -1 in base m is equivalent to allowing two digits in base m. Because m² -1 = (m-1)m + (m-1). So, any digit up to m² -1 can be represented as two digits in base m. Therefore, the number of representations of n with digits up to m² -1 in base m is equal to the number of representations of n in base m with twice as many digits. But since each "digit" in the original problem corresponds to two digits in the expanded base m system, the number of representations is equal to the product of (d_i +1) for each digit d_i in the base m expansion of n.

But when we expand each coefficient into two digits, we're effectively allowing carries over two positions. However, in the example with m=2, n=5 (101 in base 2), this would give product_{i=0}^2 (d_i +1) = (1+1)(0+1)(1+1)=2*1*2=4, which contradicts our previous finding of two valid polynomials. But wait, we have to check what the correspondence is.

Wait, if each coefficient a_i can be represented as two digits in base m, then the number of representations is the product over all base m digits of (digit +1). However, in the example, the product is 4, but we have two valid polynomials according to the mapping. However, the direct evaluation shows that there are two valid polynomials. But we initially thought there was a third, but it seems that the third is invalid due to the constraints. Therefore, the correct number might be 2 in that case, aligning with the product formula.

But this contradicts our previous direct calculation where we thought there was a third polynomial. Wait, let's check again.

The polynomial x +3: coefficients a_1=1, a_0=3. When evaluated at m=2, gives 2 +3=5. But according to the equations:

d_0 =3 mod 2=1, so c_0=1, d_0=1.

Then, for i=1: c_1 + d_0 =1 +1=2, which must equal n_1=0. This is impossible, so this polynomial does not satisfy the equations, and hence is not a valid solution. Therefore, it is not counted. Hence, there are only two valid polynomials: x² +1 and 2x +1.

Therefore, the product formula gives the correct answer of 2 for the example. The confusion arose because we incorrectly considered the polynomial x +3 as valid, but it does not satisfy the necessary constraints when the coefficients are split into c_i and d_i. Therefore, the formula product_{i ≥1} (n_i +1) is correct.

Therefore, the number of polynomials is the product of (digit +1) for each digit in the base m expansion of n, starting from the first digit (i=1) onward. 

But wait, in the example, we multiplied for i=1 and i=2, which corresponded to the digits 0 and1, giving (0 +1)*(1 +1)=1*2=2. Hence, generalizing, the number of polynomials is the product of (n_i +1) for each digit n_i in the base m expansion of n, excluding the zeroth digit. 

Therefore, the final answer is the product over all digits of n in base m (excluding the constant term) of (digit +1). Therefore, if the base m expansion of n is d_0 + d_1 m + ... + d_k m^k, then the number of polynomials is (d_1 +1)(d_2 +1)...(d_k +1).

Therefore, the answer is the product of (d_i +1) for each digit d_i in the base m expansion of n, starting from the first digit (i=1) up to the highest non-zero digit.

Thus, the number of such polynomials is the product of (d_i +1) for each digit d_i in the base m representation of n, except for the least significant digit d_0.

Therefore, the solution is to express n in base m, and then take the product of (d_i +1) for each digit d_i beyond the units place (i.e., for i ≥1).

Hence, the answer is the product of (digit +1) for each digit in the base m expansion of n except the last one.

Wait, but in the example, n=5 is 101 in base 2. The digits beyond the units place are d_1=0 and d_2=1. The product is (0 +1)*(1 +1)=1*2=2, which matches the correct number of polynomials.

Therefore, the correct general formula is the product of (d_i +1) for each digit d_i in the base m expansion of n, excluding the least significant digit (i=0).

Therefore, the number of polynomials is the product of (digit +1) for each digit in the base m representation of n starting from the first digit (i=1) to the highest digit. Hence, if n in base m is written as d_k ... d_1 d_0, then the number of polynomials is the product from i=1 to k of (d_i +1).

**Final Answer**
The number of such polynomials is the product of one more than each digit in the base \( m \) expansion of \( n \), excluding the constant term. Therefore, the answer is \(\boxed{\prod_{i=1}^{k} (d_i + 1)}\), where \( d_i \) are the digits of \( n \) in base \( m \).
</think>To solve the problem of finding the number of polynomials \( P(x) \) with coefficients from the set \(\{0, 1, \ldots, m^2 - 1\}\) such that \( P(m) = n \), we start by expressing \( n \) in its base \( m \) expansion. Let \( n \) be represented in base \( m \) as:
\[ n = d_0 + d_1 m + d_2 m^2 + \cdots + d_k m^k, \]
where each \( d_i \) is a digit in the base \( m \) system, i.e., \( 0 \leq d_i < m \).

Each coefficient \( a_i \) in the polynomial \( P(x) \) can be written as:
\[ a_i = c_i + m \cdot e_i, \]
where \( c_i \) and \( e_i \) are digits in base \( m \), i.e., \( 0 \leq c_i, e_i < m \).

When evaluating \( P(m) \), we get:
\[ P(m) = \sum_{i=0}^k a_i m^i = \sum_{i=0}^k (c_i + m \cdot e_i) m^i = \sum_{i=0}^k c_i m^i + \sum_{i=0}^k e_i m^{i+1} = c_0 + (c_1 + e_0)m + (c_2 + e_1)m^2 + \cdots. \]

This must equal the base \( m \) expansion of \( n \):
\[ n = d_0 + d_1 m + d_2 m^2 + \cdots + d_k m^k. \]

For the equality to hold, we need:
\[ c_0 = d_0, \]
\[ c_1 + e_0 = d_1, \]
\[ c_2 + e<#sep#>human: Wojtek has just won a maths competition in Byteland! The prize is admirable — a great book called 'Card Tricks for Everyone.' 'Great!' he thought, 'I can finally use this old, dusted deck of cards that's always been lying unused on my desk!'

The first chapter of the book is 'How to Shuffle k Cards in Any Order You Want.' It's basically a list of n intricate methods of shuffling the deck of k cards in a deterministic way. Specifically, the i-th recipe can be described as a permutation (P_{i,1}, P_{i,2}, ..., P_{i,k}) of integers from 1 to k. If we enumerate the cards in the deck from 1 to k from top to bottom, then P_{i,j} indicates the number of the j-th card from the top of the deck after the shuffle.

The day is short and Wojtek wants to learn only some of the tricks today. He will pick two integers l, r (1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ n), and he will memorize each trick from the l-th to the r-th, inclusive. He will then take a sorted deck of k cards and repeatedly apply random memorized tricks until he gets bored. He still likes maths, so he started wondering: how many different decks can he have after he stops shuffling it?

Wojtek still didn't choose the integers l and r, but he is still curious. Therefore, he defined f(l, r) as the number of different decks he can get if he memorizes all the tricks between the l-th and the r-th, inclusive. What is the value of

$$$∑_{l=1}^n ∑_{r=l}^n f(l, r)?$$$
Input

The first line contains two integers n, k (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ 5) — the number of tricks and the number of cards in Wojtek's deck.

Each of the following n lines describes a single trick and is described by k distinct integers P_{i,1}, P_{i,2}, ..., P_{i, k} (1 ≤ P_{i, j} ≤ k).

Output

Output the value of the sum described in the statement.

Examples

Input


3 3
2 1 3
3 1 2
1 3 2


Output


25


Input


2 4
4 1 3 2
4 3 1 2


Output


31

Note

Consider the first sample:

  * The first trick swaps two top cards. 
  * The second trick takes a card from the bottom and puts it on the top of the deck. 
  * The third trick swaps two bottom cards. 



The first or the third trick allow Wojtek to generate only two distinct decks (either the two cards are swapped or not). Therefore, f(1, 1) = f(3, 3) = 2.

The second trick allows him to shuffle the deck in a cyclic order. Therefore, f(2,2)=3.

It turns that two first tricks or two last tricks are enough to shuffle the deck in any way desired by Wojtek. Therefore, f(1,2) = f(2,3) = f(1,3) = 3! = 6.

gpt: <think> Okay, so I'm trying to solve this problem where Wojtek can choose a range of tricks between l and r and then apply them any number of times. The question is to find the sum over all possible l and r of the number of distinct decks he can get after applying those tricks.

Hmm, the key here is to figure out for each interval [l, r], what's the number of possible permutations achievable by combining the tricks in that interval any number of times. Since permutations can be composed (like applying tricks multiple times), each interval's tricks form a subgroup of the symmetric group S_k (since applying permutations multiple times and combining them via composition gives another permutation in the subgroup generated by those tricks).

The problem thus reduces to, for every possible interval [l, r], computing the size of the subgroup generated by the permutations in that interval, then summing all those sizes over all intervals.

But wait, how do we compute this efficiently? Since n can be up to 200,000, doing anything O(n²) would be impossible. We need a way to compute this in O(n) or O(n log n) time. That suggests that some dynamic programming approach might be needed, where we can track the possible subgroups incrementally as we add more permutations to the interval.

Alternatively, maybe we can model this problem by maintaining a set of possible groups, and for each new permutation, see how it interacts with previous groups. from torch import nn as nn
from torch.nn import init as init
from torchvision.models import vgg as vgg
import torch.utils.data
from torch.utils import data as data
from torch import autograd as autograd


class TSAFusion(nn.Module):
    """Temporal Spatial Attention (TSA) fusion module.

    Temporal: Calculate the correlation between center frame and
        neighboring frames;
    Spatial: It has 3 pyramid levels, the attention is similar to SFT.
        (SFT: Recovering realistic texture in image super-resolution by deep
            spatial feature transform.)

    Args:
        num_feat (int): Channel number of middle features. Default: 64.
        num_frame (int): Number of frames. Default: 5.
        center_frame_idx (int): The index of center frame. Default: 2.
    """

    def __init__(self, num_feat=64, num_frame=5, center_frame_idx=2):
        super(TSAFusion, self).__init__()
        self.center_frame_idx = center_frame_idx
        self.temporal_attn1 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 3, 1, 1)
        self.temporal_attn2 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 3, 1, 1)
        self.feat_fusion = nn.Conv2d(num_frame * num_feat, num_feat, 1, 1)
        self.max_pool = nn.MaxPool2d(3, stride=2, padding=1)
        self.avg_pool = nn.AvgPool2d(3, stride=2, padding=1)
        self.spatial_attn1 = nn.Conv2d(num_frame * num_feat, num_feat, 1)
        self.spatial_attn2 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat * 2, num_feat, 1)
        self.spatial_attn3 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 3, 1, 1)
        self.spatial_attn4 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 1)
        self.spatial_attn5 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 3, 1, 1)
        self.spatial_attn_l1 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 1)
        self.spatial_attn_l2 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat * 2, num_feat, 3, 1, 1)
        self.spatial_attn_l3 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 3, 1, 1)
        self.spatial_attn_add1 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 1)
        self.spatial_attn_add2 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 1)
        self.lrelu = nn.LeakyReLU(negative_slope=0.1, inplace=True)
        self.upsample = nn.Upsample(scale_factor=2, mode='bilinear',
            align_corners=False)

    def forward(self, aligned_feat):
        """
        Args:
            aligned_feat (Tensor): Aligned features with shape (b, t, c, h, w).

        Returns:
            Tensor: Features after TSA with the shape (b, c, h, w).
        """
        b, t, c, h, w = aligned_feat.size()
        embedding_ref = self.temporal_attn1(aligned_feat[:, self.
            center_frame_idx, :, :, :].clone())
        embedding = self.temporal_attn2(aligned_feat.view(-1, c, h, w))
        embedding = embedding.view(b, t, -1, h, w)
        corr_l = []
        for i in range(t):
            emb_neighbor = embedding[:, i, :, :, :]
            corr = torch.sum(emb_neighbor * embedding_ref, 1)
            corr_l.append(corr.unsqueeze(1))
        corr_prob = torch.sigmoid(torch.cat(corr_l, dim=1))
        corr_prob = corr_prob.unsqueeze(2).expand(b, t, c, h, w)
        corr_prob = corr_prob.contiguous().view(b, -1, h, w)
        aligned_feat = aligned_feat.view(b, -1, h, w) * corr_prob
        feat = self.lrelu(self.feat_fusion(aligned_feat))
        attn = self.lrelu(self.spatial_attn1(aligned_feat))
        attn_max = self.max_pool(attn)
        attn_avg = self.avg_pool(attn)
        attn = self.lrelu(self.spatial_attn2(torch.cat([attn_max, attn_avg],
            dim=1)))
        attn_level = self.lrelu(self.spatial_attn_l1(attn))
        attn_max = self.max_pool(attn_level)
        attn_avg = self.avg_pool(attn_level)
        attn_level = self.lrelu(self.spatial_attn_l2(torch.cat([attn_max,
            attn_avg], dim=1)))
        attn_level = self.lrelu(self.spatial_attn_l3(attn_level))
        attn_level = self.upsample(attn_level)
        attn = self.lrelu(self.spatial_attn3(attn)) + attn_level
        attn = self.lrelu(self.spatial_attn4(attn))
        attn = self.upsample(attn)
        attn = self.spatial_attn5(attn)
        attn_add = self.spatial_attn_add2(self.lrelu(self.spatial_attn_add1
            (attn)))
        attn = torch.sigmoid(attn)
        feat = feat * attn * 2 + attn_add
        return feat


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 5, 64, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]
<#sep#> e_i m^{i+1} = c_0 + (c_1 + e_0)m + (c_2 + e_1)m^2 + \cdots. \]

This must equal the base \( m \) expansion of \( n \):
\[ n = d_0 + d_1 m + d_2 m^2 + \cdots + d_k m^k. \]

For the equality to hold, we need:
\[ c_0 = d_0, \]
\[ c_1 + e_0 = d_1, \]
\[ c_2 + e_1 = d_2, \]
\[ \vdots \]
\[ c_k + e_{k-1} = d_k. \]

Each equation \( c_i + e_{i-1} = d_i \) (for \( i \geq 1 \)) can be solved by noting that \( e_{i-1} \) can take any value from 0 to \( d_i \), giving \( d_i + 1 \) choices for \( e_{i-1} \). Once \( e_{i-1} \) is chosen, \( c_i \) is determined as \( c_i = d_i - e_{i-1} \).

Thus, the number of valid choices for each \( e_{i-1} \) (for \( i \geq 1 \)) is \( d_i + 1 \). The total number of polynomials \( P(x) \) is the product of these choices for each \( d_i \) from \( d_1 \) to \( d_k \).

### Final Answer
The number of such polynomials is:
\[ \boxed{\prod_{i=1}^{k} (d_i + 1)}, \]
where \( d_i \) are the digits of \( n \) in base \( m \), excluding the constant term \( d_0 \).<#sep#> how do the starting positions l work with that.

import torch
from torch import nn
import torch.utils.data
import torch.cuda
import torch.optim


class MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):
    """
    Multi-head scaled dot-product attention layer.

    Args:
        hidden_size: size of the embeddings in the model, also known as d_model
        num_attention_heads: number of heads in multi-head attention
        attn_score_dropout: probability of dropout applied to attention scores
        attn_layer_dropout: probability of dropout applied to the output of the
            whole layer, but before layer normalization
    """

    def __init__(self, hidden_size, num_attention_heads, attn_score_dropout
        =0.0, attn_layer_dropout=0.0):
        super().__init__()
        if hidden_size % num_attention_heads != 0:
            raise ValueError(
                'The hidden size (%d) is not a multiple of the number of attention heads (%d)'
                 % (hidden_size, num_attention_heads))
        self.hidden_size = hidden_size
        self.num_attention_heads = num_attention_heads
        self.attn_head_size = int(hidden_size / num_attention_heads)
        self.attn_scale = math.sqrt(math.sqrt(self.attn_head_size))
        self.query_net = nn.Linear(hidden_size, hidden_size)
        self.key_net = nn.Linear(hidden_size, hidden_size)
        self.value_net = nn.Linear(hidden_size, hidden_size)
        self.out_projection = nn.Linear(hidden_size, hidden_size)
        self.attn_dropout = nn.Dropout(attn_score_dropout)
        self.layer_dropout = nn.Dropout(attn_layer_dropout)

    def transpose_for_scores(self, x):
        new_x_shape = x.size()[:-1] + (self.num_attention_heads, self.
            attn_head_size)
        x = x.view(*new_x_shape)
        return x.permute(0, 2, 1, 3)

    def forward(self, queries, keys, values, attention_mask):
        query = self.query_net(queries)
        key = self.key_net(keys)
        value = self.value_net(values)
        query = self.transpose_for_scores(query) / self.attn_scale
        key = self.transpose_for_scores(key) / self.attn_scale
        value = self.transpose_for_scores(value)
        attention_scores = torch.matmul(query, key.transpose(-1, -2))
        if attention_mask is not None:
            attention_scores = attention_scores + attention_mask
        attention_probs = torch.softmax(attention_scores, dim=-1)
        attention_probs = self.attn_dropout(attention_probs)
        context = torch.matmul(attention_probs, value)
        context = context.permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous()
        new_context_shape = context.size()[:-2] + (self.hidden_size,)
        context = context.view(*new_context_shape)
        output_states = self.out_projection(context)
        output_states = self.layer_dropout(output_states)
        return output_states


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4,
        4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'hidden_size': 4, 'num_attention_heads': 4}]

  <#sep#>import torch
from torchvision.transforms import *


class ConvBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_size, output_size, kernel_size=3, stride=1,
        padding=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(ConvBlock, self).__init__()
        self.conv = torch.nn.Conv2d(input_size, output_size, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, bias=bias)
        self.norm = norm
        if self.norm == 'batch':
            self.bn = torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(output_size)
        elif self.norm == 'instance':
            self.bn = torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(output_size)
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.norm is not None:
            out = self.bn(self.conv(x))
        else:
            out = self.conv(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            return self.act(out)
        else:
            return out


class DeconvBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_size, output_size, kernel_size=4, stride=2,
        padding=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(DeconvBlock, self).__init__()
        self.deconv = torch.nn.ConvTranspose2d(input_size, output_size,
            kernel_size, stride, padding, bias=bias)
        self.norm = norm
        if self.norm == 'batch':
            self.bn = torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(output_size)
        elif self.norm == 'instance':
            self.bn = torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(output_size)
        self.activation = activation
        if self.activation == 'relu':
            self.act = torch.nn.ReLU(True)
        elif self.activation == 'prelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.PReLU()
        elif self.activation == 'lrelu':
            self.act = torch.nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, True)
        elif self.activation == 'tanh':
            self.act = torch.nn.Tanh()
        elif self.activation == 'sigmoid':
            self.act = torch.nn.Sigmoid()

    def forward(self, x):
        if self.norm is not None:
            out = self.bn(self.deconv(x))
        else:
            out = self.deconv(x)
        if self.activation is not None:
            return self.act(out)
        else:
            return out


class D_UpBlock(torch.nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, num_filter, kernel_size=8, stride=4, padding=2,
        num_stages=1, bias=True, activation='prelu', norm=None):
        super(D_UpBlock, self).__init__()
        self.conv = ConvBlock(num_filter * num_stages, num_filter, 1, 1, 0,
            activation, norm=None)
        self.up_conv1 = DeconvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)
        self.up_conv2 = ConvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)
        self.up_conv3 = DeconvBlock(num_filter, num_filter, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, activation, norm=None)

    def forward(self, x):
        x = self.conv(x)
        h0 = self.up_conv1(x)
        l0 = self.up_conv2(h0)
        h1 = self.up_conv3(l0 - x)
        return h1 + h0


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'num_filter': 4}]

Wait, let me think more carefully. At step r, we can consider that the current permutation p can be added to previous intervals:

- Any subgroup G present at step r−1 can be combined with the new permutation to form ⟨ G, p_r ⟩. This new subgroup will form part of the next step (r) if we choose to include the new permutation with the previous interval.

- Additionally, the subgroup formed by only p_r (starting at l = r) is also a new possibility at step r.

So the recurrence for the problem's counting function can be thought of as:

At step r, the new_subgroups for intervals up to r are:

   The combination of existing subgroups (from step r−1) merged with p_r, plus the subgroup from p_r alone.

To track counts, since for each subgroup H_new obtained from merging G_prev with p_r, the count of H_new is the count of G_prev (the number of intervals which, when combined with p_r, form H_new) plus the count for the new subgroup starting at l=r alone.

At step r:

   The count[r] can be computed as:

       a) The subgroup formed by p_r (the interval [r, r]). Its count is initially 1.

       b) For each previous subgroup G_prev in count[r-1], the subgroup generated when adding p_r to G_prev (call it H = ⟨ G_prev, p_r ⟩ ). For each such H, we add to count[r][H] the count[r-1][G_prev].

   Additionally, the interval [r..r], which doesn't extend any previous interval, contributes to the subgroup generated by p alone.

   Therefore:

   For all G_prev groups in step r-1, any of the intervals that had G_prev (counted in count[r-1][G_prev]) can contribute by forming new intervals ending at r (their starting l is the same as before), leading to H = ⟨ G_prev, p_r ⟩.

   So the contribution for H from previous steps is sum_{G_prev in subgroup(s.t. ⟨ G_prev, p_r ⟩ = H)} count[r-1][G_prev]

   Additionally, the new group formed by [r..r], which is just ⟨p_r⟩. The count for this group is 1 (the interval [r..r]).

Thus, the total count for each subgroup H in current step r is the sum over all G_prev such that ⟨G_prev, p_r⟩ == H of the count for G_prev from step r-1, plus 1 if H is exactly the group generated by [r..r].

Hence, this recurrence is the way to model the count.

from torch import nn as nn
from torch.nn import init as init
from torch.nn.modules.batchnorm import _BatchNorm
from torchvision.models import vgg as vgg
import torch.utils.data
from torch.utils import data as data
from torch import autograd as autograd


@torch.no_grad()
def default_init_weights(module_list, scale=1, bias_fill=0, **kwargs):
    """Initialize network weights.

    Args:
        module_list (list[nn.Module] | nn.Module): Modules to be initialized.
        scale (float): Scale initialized weights, especially for residual
            blocks. Default: 1.
        bias_fill (float): The value to fill bias. Default: 0
        kwargs (dict): Other arguments for initialization function.
    """
    if not isinstance(module_list, list):
        module_list = [module_list]
    for module in module_list:
        for m in module.modules():
            if isinstance(m, nn.Conv2d):
                init.kaiming_normal_(m.weight, **kwargs)
                m.weight.data *= scale
                if m.bias is not None:
                    m.bias.data.fill_(bias_fill)
            elif isinstance(m, nn.Linear):
                init.kaiming_normal_(m.weight, **kwargs)
                m.weight.data *= scale
                if m.bias is not None:
                    m.bias.data.fill_(bias_fill)
            elif isinstance(m, _BatchNorm):
                init.constant_(m.weight, 1)
                if m.bias is not None:
                    m.bias.data.fill_(bias_fill)


class ResidualBlockNoBN(nn.Module):
    """Residual block without BN.

    It has a style of:
        ---Conv-ReLU-Conv-+-
         |________________|

    Args:
        num_feat (int): Channel number of intermediate features.
            Default: 64.
        res_scale (float): Residual scale. Default: 1.
        pytorch_init (bool): If set to True, use pytorch default init,
            otherwise, use default_init_weights. Default: False.
    """

    def __init__(self, num_feat=64, res_scale=1, pytorch_init=False):
        super(ResidualBlockNoBN, self).__init__()
        self.res_scale = res_scale
        self.conv1 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 3, 1, 1, bias=True)
        self.conv2 = nn.Conv2d(num_feat, num_feat, 3, 1, 1, bias=True)
        self.relu = nn.ReLU(inplace=True)
        if not pytorch_init:
            default_init_weights([self.conv1, self.conv2], 0.1)

    def forward(self, x):
        identity = x
        out = self.conv2(self.relu(self.conv1(x)))
        return identity + out * self.res_scale


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 64, 64, 64])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

Then, the contribution of step r to the total sum S is the sum over all H in count[r] of (count[r][H] * size(H)), and the total S is the sum over each step's contribution.

Now, the remaining problem is to compute this efficiently given the number of groups is manageable.

The key steps to implement this are:

1. Represent subgroups in a hashable way (like as a tuple of the elements, or a bitmask). 

2. Precompute all elements of Sₖ, and map each permutations' ordinal to an id (since all given permutations are in Sₖ).

3. For each permutation p_r (as a permutation in Sₖ), compute G_r (the subgroup ⟨p_r⟩ alone).

4. At each step r, for each subgroup G_prev from the previous step, compute H_new = ⟨ G_prev, p_r ⟩, and accumulate counts.

The problem arises in how to compute ⟨ G_prev, p_r ⟩ every time. To compute not ⟨ G_prev ∪ {p_r}⟩ but the subgroup generated by the combination.

This computation is fundamental. Here's how to do it efficiently:

Let's first note that for each current G_prev subgroup, to compute the subgroup generated by adding p_r, we can formally compute the closure of G_prev with the new permutation p_r.

To do this efficiently:

   To compute ⟨ G_prev, p_r ⟩, we can start with all elements of G_prev and the permutation p_r. Then, we need to generate all elements you get by multiplying these together until closure.

from torch import nn
import torch.nn
import torch.autograd


def sparse_bmm(sparse_matrix, dense_matrix_batch):
    """
    Perform torch.bmm on an unbatched sparse matrix and a batched dense matrix.

    Args:
        sparse_matrix (torch.sparse.FloatTensor): Shape = (m, n)
        dense_matrix_batch (torch.FloatTensor): Shape = (b, n, p)

    Returns:
        (torch.FloatTensor):
            Result of the batched matrix multiplication. Shape = (b, n, p)
    """
    m = sparse_matrix.shape[0]
    b, n, p = dense_matrix_batch.shape
    dense_matrix = dense_matrix_batch.transpose(0, 1).reshape(n, b * p)
    result = torch.sparse.mm(sparse_matrix, dense_matrix)
    return result.reshape(m, b, p).transpose(0, 1)


class GraphConv(nn.Module):
    """A simple graph convolution layer, similar to the one defined by *Kipf et al.* in
    `Semi-Supervised Classification with Graph Convolutional Networks`_ ICLR 2017

    This operation with self_layer=False is equivalent to :math:`(A H W)` where:
    - :math:`H` is the node features with shape (batch_size, num_nodes, input_dim)
    - :math:`W` is a weight matrix of shape (input_dim, output_dim)
    - :math:`A` is the adjacency matrix of shape (num_nodes, num_nodes).
    It can include self-loop.

    With normalize_adj=True, it is equivalent to :math:`(D^{-1} A H W)`, where:
    - :math:`D` is a diagonal matrix with :math:`D_{ii}` = the sum of the i-th row of A.
    In other words, :math:`D` is the incoming degree of each node.

    With self_layer=True, it is equivalent to the above plus :math:`(H W_{\\text{self}})`, where:
    - :math:`W_{\\text{self}}` is a separate weight matrix to filter each node's self features.

    Note that when self_layer is True, A should not include self-loop.

    Args:
        input_dim (int): The number of features in each input node.
        output_dim (int): The number of features in each output node.
        bias (bool): Whether to add bias after the node-wise linear layer.

    Example:
        >>> node_feat = torch.rand(1, 3, 5)
        >>> i = torch.LongTensor(
        ...     [[0, 1, 1, 2, 2, 0], [1, 0, 2, 1, 0, 2]])
        >>> v = torch.FloatTensor([1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1])
        >>> adj = torch.sparse.FloatTensor(i, v, torch.Size([3, 3]))
        >>> model = GraphConv(5, 10)
        >>> output = model(node_feat, adj)
        >>> # pre-normalize adj
        >>> adj = normalize_adj(adj)
        >>> output = model(node_feat, adj, normalize_adj=False)

    .. _Semi-Supervised Classification with Graph Convolutional Networks:
        https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.02907
    """

    def __init__(self, input_dim, output_dim, self_layer=True, bias=True):
        super(GraphConv, self).__init__()
        self.self_layer = self_layer
        self.linear = nn.Linear(input_dim, output_dim, bias=bias)
        if self_layer:
            self.linear_self = nn.Linear(input_dim, output_dim, bias=bias)
        else:
            self.linear_self = None
        self.initialize()

    def initialize(self):
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.linear.weight.data)
        if self.linear.bias is not None:
            self.linear.bias.data.uniform_(-1.0, 1.0)
        if self.self_layer:
            nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.linear_self.weight.data)
            if self.linear_self.bias is not None:
                self.linear_self.bias.data.uniform_(-1.0, 1.0)

    def forward(self, node_feat, adj, normalize_adj=True):
        """
        Args:
            node_feat (torch.FloatTensor):
                Shape = (batch_size, num_nodes, input_dim)
                The input features of each node.
            adj (torch.sparse.FloatTensor or torch.FloatTensor):
                Shape = (num_nodes, num_nodes)
                The adjacency matrix. adj[i, j] is non-zero if there's an
                incoming edge from j to i. Should not include self-loop if
                self_layer is True.
            normalize_adj (bool):
                Set this to true to apply normalization to adjacency; that is,
                each output feature will be divided by the number of incoming
                neighbors. If normalization is not desired, or if the adjacency
                matrix is pre-normalized, set this to False to improve
                performance.

        Returns:
            (torch.FloatTensor):
                The output features of each node.
                Shape = (batch_size, num_nodes, output_dim)
        """
        if adj.type().endswith('sparse.FloatTensor'):
            if normalize_adj:
                norm = torch.sparse.mm(adj, torch.ones((adj.shape[0], 1),
                    device=node_feat.device))
                result = sparse_bmm(adj, self.linear(node_feat)) / norm
            else:
                result = sparse_bmm(adj, self.linear(node_feat))
        elif normalize_adj:
            norm = torch.matmul(adj, torch.ones((adj.shape[0], 1), device=
                node_feat.device))
            result = torch.matmul(adj, self.linear(node_feat)) / norm
        else:
            result = torch.matmul(adj, self.linear(node_feat))
        if self.self_layer:
            result += self.linear_self(node_feat)
        return result


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'input_dim': 4, 'output_dim': 4}]
